A System of Interaction and Structure III: The Complexity of System BV and Pomset Logic \rsuper * Thanks: L. T. D. Nguyễn was supported by the LambdaComb project (ANR-21-CE48-0017) while working at École polytechnique and by the LABEX MILYON (ANR-10-LABX-0070) of Université de Lyon, within the program “Investissements d’Avenir” operated by the French National Research Agency (ANR)
Lê Thành Dũng (Tito) Nguyễn ORCID:0000-0002-6900-5577, Lutz Straßburger ORCID:0000-0003-4661-6540

Abstract
. Pomset logic and 𝖡𝖵 {\mathsf{BV}} are both logics that extend multiplicative linear logic (with mix) with a third connective that is self-dual and non-commutative. Whereas pomset logic originates from the study of coherence spaces and proof nets, 𝖡𝖵 {\mathsf{BV}} originates from the study of series-parallel orders, cographs, and proof systems. Both logics enjoy a cut-admissibility result, but for neither logic can this be done in the sequent calculus. Provability in pomset logic can be checked via a proof net correctness criterion and in 𝖡𝖵 {\mathsf{BV}} via a deep inference proof system. It has long been conjectured that these two logics are the same. In this paper we show that this conjecture is false. We also investigate the complexity of the two logics, exhibiting a huge gap between the two. Whereas provability in 𝖡𝖵 {\mathsf{BV}} is 𝐍𝐏 \mathbf{NP} -complete, provability in Pomset logic is 𝚺 𝟐 𝐩 \mathbf{\Sigma_{2}^{p}} -complete. We also make some observations with respect to possible sequent systems for the two logics.

Introduction

There are two ways to put this paper into perspective. First, it is
the third paper in a series of five papers: the first
two
[
Gug07
,
Tiu06
]
introduce a logic called

system
{\mathsf{BV}}
, prove cut elimination
for it, and show that
deep inference
is necessary for having a
cut-free deductive system for
{\mathsf{BV}}
; and the last
two
[
SG11
,
GS11
]
study system
\mathsf{NEL}
, a conservative extension
of
{\mathsf{BV}}
with the exponentials of linear logic. The second way to look
at this paper is that it finally answers the longstanding open
question whether
{\mathsf{BV}}
and pomset logic
[
Ret21
]
are the
same.

Pomset logic has been discovered by Christian
Retoré
[
Ret93
,
Ret97a
]
through the study of coherence spaces
which form a semantics of proofs for linear logic
[
Gir87
]
, by
observing
1
1

1

According to Retoré
(cf.
http://www.lirmm.fr/~retore/PRESENTATIONS/pomset4michele.pdf
), this
semantic observation is due to Girard.

that next to the two operations
\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}
(
tensor
or

multiplicative conjunction
) and
\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}

multiplicative disjunction
) on coherence spaces there are two other operations

\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}
and
\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleright$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleright$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleright$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleright$}}}
, which are non-commutative, obey

, and are self-dual, i.e.,
\langle{A\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}B}\rangle^{\bot}={A^{\bot}\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}B^{\bot}}
.
Furthermore, there are canonical linear maps from

A\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}B
to
A\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}B
, and from
A\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}B
to
A\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}B
. From this
semantic observation, Retoré derived a a
proof net
syntax, i.e., a graph-like
representation of proofs together with a combinatorial
correctness
criterion
that distinguishes actual proofs among the considered space of
graphs, together with a cut elimination theorem. However, he
could not provide a sound and complete cut-free sequent calculus for
this logic.

System
{\mathsf{BV}}
was found by Alessio Guglielmi
[
Gug07
,
Gug99
]
2
2

2

The initial ideas of the inference rules have also been present in
[
Ret99b
]
even though not formulated as a proof system admitting cut-elimination.
through a
syntactic investigation of the connectives of pomset logic and a graph
theoretic study of series-parallel orders and cographs. The difficulty
of presenting this combination of commutative and non-commutative
connectives in the sequent calculus triggered the development of the

calculus of structures

[
GS01
]
, the first proper deep inference proof
formalism.
3
3

3

An introductory survey on deep inference can be found in
[
ATS19
]
.

This leads to the strange situation that we have two logics, pomset
logic and
{\mathsf{BV}}
, which are both conservative extensions of
multiplicative linear logic with mix (
\mathord{\mathsf{MLL\!+\!mix}}
)
[
FR94
]
with a noncommutative
connective
\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}
such that
A\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}B\vdash A\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}B
and
A\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}B\vdash A\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}B
,
and which both obey a cut elimination result—the only difference
being that pomset logic only had a proof net syntax but no deductive
proof system, and
{\mathsf{BV}}
only had a deductive proof system but no proof
nets. This naturally led to the conjecture that both logics are the
same
[
Str03b
]
.

In this paper we show that this conjecture is false. It can easily be
shown
[
Str03b
,
Str03a
,
Ret99b
]
that every theorem in

{\mathsf{BV}}
is also a theorem of pomset logic, and for the sake of clarity,
we also give a proof in this paper (in
Section
3.1
). However, the converse is not true, and
we give an example of a formula that is a theorem of pomset logic but
not provable in
{\mathsf{BV}}
(in Section
3.2
).

This naturally leads to the question of complexity. Do both logics
have the same or different complexity? It has been observed
in
[
Kah08
]
that provability in
{\mathsf{BV}}
is
\mathbf{NP}
-complete. The reason
is that
\mathbf{NP}
-hardness is inherited from
\mathord{\mathsf{MLL\!+\!mix}}
, and containment in

\mathbf{NP}
follows from the fact that the size of every proof in
{\mathsf{BV}}
is
polynomial in the size of the conclusion, and the correctness of such
a proof can be checked in time which is linear in the size of the
proof (see Section
4.2
for more details). However,
provability in pomset logic is
\mathbf{\Sigma_{2}^{p}}
-complete. Even though the
size of a pomset logic proof net is polynomial in the size of its
conclusion, checking correctness of such a proof net is

\mathbf{coNP}
-complete. The details for these results are discussed in
Sections
4.3
–
4.5
.

This complexity result explains why it is impossible to give a
deductive proof system (in the sense of Cook and
Reckhow
[
CR79
]
) for pomset logic. Nonetheless, there
is a recent proposal by Slavnov
[
Sla19
]
for a decorated
sequent calculus for pomset logic. We look at this calculus in
Section
5.4
and relate it to our complexity
result. Before that, we show in Section
5.1
that the
sequent calculus with cut, that Retoré proposed for pomset logic is in
fact a sound and complete sequent calculus for
{\mathsf{BV}}
.
4
4

4

In
fact, in various publications on pomset logic, Retoré proposes
different sequent calculi (one in his PhD thesis

[
Ret93
, Chapitre 8]
, one in

[
Ret97a
, Section 7]
, and more recently another on
in
[
Ret21
]
); we work here with the one
in
[
Ret21
]
.

In summary, the paper makes the following contributions:

•

In Section
2
, we give a gentle and easy
accessible introduction to pomset logic and
{\mathsf{BV}}
. We unify the
notation and terminology, and present some important properties,
that will be needed in later sections.

•

In Section
3
, we are showing that
{\mathsf{BV}}
is properly contained in pomset logic, by showing that every theorem of
{\mathsf{BV}}
is a theorem of pomset logic, but not vice versa. These results have already been presented in
[
NS22
]
, of which this article is an extended version.

•

In Section
4
, we are discussing the complexity of pomset logic and
{\mathsf{BV}}
. More precisely, we are recalling Kahramanoğulları’s result on the
\mathbf{NP}
-completeness of
{\mathsf{BV}}
, and we show that checking correctness of a pomset logic proof is

\mathbf{coNP}
-complete and that provability in pomset logic is
\mathbf{\Sigma_{2}^{p}}
-complete.

•

Finally, in Section
5
, we come back to the sequent calculus, discussing the difficulties that the two logics pose, and how they can or cannot be overcome.

2.
Preliminaries on Pomset Logic and
{\mathsf{BV}}

In this section we will introduce the the two logics,
{\mathsf{BV}}
and pomset
logic, together with some basic underlying graph theoretical and proof
theoretical concepts. Even though pomset logic was discovered through
the study of coherence semantics, we will here only discuss its
syntax, as coherence spaces are not needed for the results of this
paper.

2.1.
Formulas, Duality and Sequents

The
formulas
of pomset logic and
{\mathsf{BV}}
are in this paper denoted by
capital Latin letters
A,B,C,\ldots
and are generated from propositional
variables
a,b,c,\ldots
, their
duals

a^{\bot},b^{\bot},c^{\bot}\ldots
and
the
unit

\mathord{\mathbb{I}}
via the three binary connectives
tensor

\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}
,
par

\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}
, and
seq

\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}
. We shall also be led to
consider terms built with those connectives whose leaves are taken in arbitrary
sets, which justifies the following more general definition.

{defi}

The
generalized formulas
over the set
X
are generated by the grammar

A,B\quad\mathrel{\raise 0.4pt\hbox{::}{=}}\quad\mathord{\mathbb{I}}\mid x\mid({A\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}B})\mid[{A\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}B}]\mid\langle{A\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}B}\rangle\qquad\text{where}\ x\in X

A generalized formula is
linear
when it contains at most one
occurrence of each
x\in X
.

We fix a countable set
{\mathcal{V}}=\{a,b,c,\ldots\}
of
propositional
variables
. To each variable
a
we injectively associate a
dual

a^{\perp}
; we write
{\mathcal{V}}^{\bot}=\{a^{\bot}\mid a\in{\mathcal{V}}\}
, and require
that
{\mathcal{V}}\cap{\mathcal{V}}^{\bot}=\varnothing
. An
atom
is either a
variable (
positive
atom) or the dual of a variable (
negative

atom). A
formula
is a generalized formula over the set of atoms
{\mathcal{V}}\cup{\mathcal{V}}^{\bot}
.

{defi}

The
size
of a generalized formula
A
over the set
X
, denoted by

\left|A\right|
, is the number of occurrences of elements of
X
in
A
.

For better readability of large formulas, we use here different kinds
of parentheses for the different connectives.
5
5

5

Note that this
is redundant and carries no additional meaning. The only use is
better readability.
In the following, we omit outermost
parentheses for better readability.

{defi}

We define the
relation
\equiv
on generalized formulas
to be the smallest
congruence generated by the rules of
Figure

1
. Those correspond to
associativity of
\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}},\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}},\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}
, commutativity of
\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}},\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}
, and
unit equations (
\mathord{\mathbb{I}}
behaves as unit for all three connectives).

Figure 1
.
The equations defining
\equiv
.

As usual in the linear logic tradition, negation is defined not as a connective
but as a mapping from formulas to formulas. Note that this does not make sense for
generalized formulas.

{defi}

The involutive
(linear) negation
or
duality

(-)^{\bot}
is
extended from propositional variables to formulas by taking De Morgan’s laws
as its inductive definition:

(a^{\bot})^{\bot}=a\hskip 13.99995pt\mathord{\mathbb{I}}^{\bot}=\mathord{\mathbb{I}}\hskip 13.99995pt({A\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}B})^{\bot}={A^{\bot}\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}B^{\bot}}\hskip 13.99995pt[{A\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}B}]^{\bot}={A^{\bot}\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}B^{\bot}}\quad\langle{A\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}B}\rangle^{\bot}={A^{\bot}\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}B^{\bot}}

The first clause means that linear negation defines a fixed-point-free
involution on the set of atoms. The last clause is what we mean when we say
that seq is
self-dual
; note that the right-hand side is indeed

{A^{\bot}\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}B^{\bot}}
and not
{B^{\bot}\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}A^{\bot}}
.
6
6

6

In that respect,
pomset logic and
{\mathsf{BV}}
are different from other non-commutative variants of
linear logic where
\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}
and
\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}
are non-commutative with
(A\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}B)^{\bot}=B^{\bot}\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}A^{\bot}
, see
Section

6.1
.

We will also need the notion of
sequent
in pomset logic. While traditional
sequent calculi use multisets
7
7

7

This holds for commutative logics.
Indeed, using multisets is equivalent to using lists and adding the exchange
rule to the system.
of formulas as sequents, pomset logic is thus named
because its sequents are
p
artially
o
rdered

m
ulti
sets
of formulas. While Retoré’s early
work
[
Ret93
,
Ret97a
]
involved arbitrary partial orders, we
consider here the simplified version from
[
Ret21
]
where sequents are
equipped with
series-parallel
orders. We shall define those orders in the
next subsection (see Proposition
10
and Remark
11
); for
now, let us just say that those orders admit a syntactic description that we
give here.

{defi}

We denote a
sequent
in pomset logic by capital Greek letters

\Gamma,\Delta,\ldots
and they are generated as follows:

\Gamma,\Delta\quad\mathrel{\raise 0.4pt\hbox{::}{=}}\quad\varnothing\mid A\mid{[\Gamma,\Delta]}\mid{\langle\Gamma;\Delta\rangle}

where
\varnothing
stands for the empty sequent and
A
can be any formula. As before, we use different
kinds of brackets for better readability.
Furthermore, we consider sequents equal modulo commutativity of

{[\cdot,\cdot]}
and associativity of
{[\cdot,\cdot]}
and

{\langle\cdot;\cdot\rangle}
, and the unit laws for the empty sequent:

\begin{array}[]{c}{[\Gamma,\Delta]}={[\Delta,\Gamma]}\qquad{[\Gamma,\varnothing]}=\Gamma\qquad{\langle\Gamma;\varnothing\rangle}=\Gamma={\langle\varnothing;\Gamma\rangle}\\[4.30554pt]
{[\Gamma,{[\Delta,\Lambda]}]}={[\Gamma,\Delta,\Lambda]}={[{[\Gamma,\Delta]},\Lambda]}\qquad{\langle\Gamma;{\langle\Delta;\Lambda\rangle}\rangle}={\langle\Gamma;\Delta;\Lambda\rangle}={\langle{\langle\Gamma;\Delta\rangle};\Lambda\rangle}\end{array}

In the remainder of this paper we will always omit redundant brackets.

{defi}

A sequent is called
flat
iff it does not contain the

{\langle\cdot;\cdot\rangle}
constructor. Therefore, flat sequents can be described by
multisets of formulas.

Remark 1
.

Pomset logic is not the only system that features
“non-flat”
sequents
with two distinct connectives. Another famous example is the logic

\mathsf{BI}
of bunched implications
[
OP99
]
.

The operations
{[\cdot,\cdot]}
and
{\langle\cdot;\cdot\rangle}
serve as
counterparts on sequents to the connectives
\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}
and
\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}
on formulas
(just as the sequent
\vdash A,B,C
morally means
A\lor B\lor C
in classical
logic). It is then natural to define a collapse operation
8
8

8

This can be seen as
the multiplication of the monad that sends
X
to the generalized formulas
over
X
.
from sequents into formulas.

{defi}

We say that a formula
A

corresponds
to a sequent
\Gamma
when:

•

either
\Gamma=A
;

•

or, inductively, there exist
B
and
C
that correspond respectively to

\Delta
and
\Lambda
such that

–

either
A={B\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}C}
and
\Gamma={[\Delta,\Lambda]}
;

–

or
A={B\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}C}
and
\Gamma={\langle\Delta;\Lambda\rangle}
.

We shall also say that
\Gamma
corresponds to
A
in this case.

For instance,
{a\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}a^{\bot}}
and
{a^{\bot}\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}a}
both correspond to

{[a,a^{\bot}]}
, but any formula also corresponds to itself as a singleton
sequent. Working modulo
\equiv
, we have:

Proposition 2
.

If the formulas
A
and
B
both correspond to a sequent
\Gamma
, then

A\equiv B
.

In the other direction, the same formula may correspond to multiple sequents.
That is no longer the case, however, for a more restricted relation between

“generalized sequents”
over a set
X
, i.e.
\Gamma,\Delta\mathrel{\raise 0.4pt\hbox{::}{=}}\varnothing\mid X\mid{[\Gamma,\Delta]}\mid{\langle\Gamma;\Delta\rangle}
modulo the same equations as
Definition
2.1
, and generalized
\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}
-free formulas modulo

\equiv
over
X
. This relation, obtained by replacing the base case of the
inductive Definition
8
by
“
\Gamma=x
for

x\in X
”
, is bijective. When
X
is taken to be the set of usual formulas,
this gives us:

Proposition 3
.

A sequent is the same as a generalized
\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}
-free formula over the
set of formulas,
modulo
\equiv
, writing
\varnothing
,

{[\Gamma,\Delta]}
and
{\langle\Gamma;\Delta\rangle}
instead of
\mathord{\mathbb{I}}
,

\Gamma\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}\Delta
and
\Gamma\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}\Delta
respectively.

2.2.
Dicographs, Relation Webs and Series-Parallel Orders

In
[
Ret97a
]
, Retoré presents proof nets for pomset logic as

RB-digraphs
, that is,
directed
graphs
equipped with perfect matchings, extending his reformulation of
\mathord{\mathsf{MLL\!+\!mix}}
proof
nets as
undirected
RB-graphs
[
Ret03
]
. We recall these notions
below.

{defi}

A
digraph

\mathcal{G}=(V_{\mathcal{G}},R_{\mathcal{G}})

consists of a finite set of
vertices

V_{\mathcal{G}}
and a set
of
edges

R_{\mathcal{G}}\subseteq V_{\mathcal{G}}^{2}\setminus\{(u,u)\mid u\in V_{\mathcal{G}}\}
. A
labeled digraph
is a digraph

\mathcal{G}
equipped with a map

\ell\colon V_{\mathcal{G}}\to{\mathcal{L}}
assigning each vertex
v
of
V_{\mathcal{G}}
a

label

\ell(v)\in{\mathcal{L}}
in the
label set

{\mathcal{L}}
. If

{\mathcal{L}}
is the set
{\mathcal{V}}\cup{\mathcal{V}}^{\bot}
of atoms,
we speak of an
atom-labeled
digraph.

{defi}

An
isomorphism
between two digraphs
\mathcal{G}
and
\mathcal{H}
is a bijection on
vertices
f:V_{\mathcal{G}}\xrightarrow{\;\sim\;}V_{\mathcal{H}}
such that
f(R_{\mathcal{G}})=R_{\mathcal{H}}
, where

f(R_{\mathcal{G}})=\{(f(u),f(v))\mid(u,v)\in R_{\mathcal{G}}\}
. As usual, if such an
f

exists,
\mathcal{G}
and
\mathcal{H}
are said to be
isomorphic
. Furthermore, if

\mathcal{G}
and
\mathcal{H}
are endowed with the vertex labelings
\ell_{\mathcal{G}}
and

\ell_{\mathcal{H}}
respectively, and
\ell_{\mathcal{G}}=\ell_{\mathcal{H}}\circ f
, then
we say that
f
is an
isomorphism of labeled digraphs
.

{defi}

For a given digraph
\mathcal{G}=(V_{\mathcal{G}},R_{\mathcal{G}})
we define the following four sets:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}R^{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.36165pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}}_{\mathcal{G}}&=&\{(u,v)\mid(u,v)\in R_{\mathcal{G}}\mbox{ and }(v,u)\in R_{\mathcal{G}}\}\\[4.30554pt]
R^{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.36165pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}}_{\mathcal{G}}&=&\{(u,v)\mid(u,v)\in R_{\mathcal{G}}\mbox{ and }(v,u)\notin R_{\mathcal{G}}\}\\[4.30554pt]
R^{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleright$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleright$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.36165pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleright$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}_{\mathcal{G}}&=&\{(u,v)\mid(u,v)\notin R_{\mathcal{G}}\mbox{ and }(v,u)\in R_{\mathcal{G}}\}\\[4.30554pt]
R^{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.36165pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}}_{\mathcal{G}}&=&\{(u,v)\mid(u,v)\notin R_{\mathcal{G}}\mbox{ and }(v,u)\notin R_{\mathcal{G}}\mbox{ and }u\neq v\}\\[4.30554pt]
\end{array}

Note that these sets can be seen as binary relations and as sets of
edges in the graph. We will use them interchangeably in both settings.

Proposition 4
.

For every digraph
\mathcal{G}=(V_{\mathcal{G}},R_{\mathcal{G}})
we have
that the relations
R^{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.36165pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}}_{\mathcal{G}}
,
R^{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.36165pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}}_{\mathcal{G}}
,
R^{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleright$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleright$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.36165pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleright$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}_{\mathcal{G}}
,

R^{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.36165pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}}_{\mathcal{G}}
are pairwise disjoint and

R^{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.36165pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}}_{\mathcal{G}}\cup R^{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.36165pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}}_{\mathcal{G}}\cup R^{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleright$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleright$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.36165pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleright$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}_{\mathcal{G}}\cup R^{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.36165pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}}_{\mathcal{G}}=V_{\mathcal{G}}\times V_{\mathcal{G}}\setminus\{(u,u)\mid u\in V_{\mathcal{G}}\}
. Furthermore, we have that
(u,v)\in R^{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.36165pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}}_{\mathcal{G}}

iff
(v,u)\in R^{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleright$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleright$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.36165pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleright$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}_{\mathcal{G}}
.

Remark 5
.

Conversely, a set
V
and four binary relations
R^{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.36165pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}},R^{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.36165pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}},R^{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleright$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleright$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.36165pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleright$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleright$}}}},R^{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.36165pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}}
, such that

(1)

R^{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.36165pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}},R^{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.36165pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}},R^{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleright$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleright$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.36165pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleright$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleright$}}}},R^{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.36165pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}}
are pairwise disjoint,

(2)

R^{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.36165pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}}
and
R^{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.36165pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}}
are symmetric,

(3)

R^{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.36165pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}}=(R^{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleright$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleright$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.36165pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleright$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleright$}}}})^{-1}
,

(4)

R^{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.36165pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}}\cup R^{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.36165pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}}\cup R^{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleright$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleright$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.36165pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleright$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}\cup R^{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.36165pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}}=V\times V\setminus\{(v,v)\mid v\in V\}

uniquely determine a digraph
(V,R^{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.36165pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}}\cup R^{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.36165pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}})
.

{nota}

Following
[
Ret97a
]
, when drawing digraphs, we use
(red/regular) arrows to denote edges in
R^{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.36165pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}}_{\mathcal{G}}
(and

R^{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleright$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleright$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.36165pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleright$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}_{\mathcal{G}}
), and arrow-free edges for
R^{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.36165pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}}_{\mathcal{G}}
. For

R^{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.36165pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}}_{\mathcal{G}}
we use no lines at all, as in
the following five examples:

a
b
c
d

a
b
c
d

a
b
c
d

a
b
c
d

a
b
c
d

(1)

This allows us to see
(V_{\mathcal{G}},R^{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.36165pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}}_{\mathcal{G}})
and

(V_{\mathcal{G}},R^{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.36165pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}}_{\mathcal{G}})
as undirected graphs.

Let us now relate (generalized) formulas and digraphs.

{defi}

Let
\mathcal{G}=(V_{\mathcal{G}},R_{\mathcal{G}})
and

\mathcal{H}=(V_{\mathcal{H}},R_{\mathcal{H}})
be
disjoint
digraphs (i.e.
V_{\mathcal{G}}\cap V_{\mathcal{H}}=\varnothing
). We can define the following operations that
correspond to the connectives of pomset logic and
{\mathsf{BV}}
:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}{\mathcal{G}\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}\mathcal{H}}&=&(V_{\mathcal{G}}\cup V_{\mathcal{H}},\;R_{\mathcal{G}}\cup R_{\mathcal{H}})\\[4.30554pt]
{\mathcal{G}\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}\mathcal{H}}&=&(V_{\mathcal{G}}\cup V_{\mathcal{H}},\;R_{\mathcal{G}}\cup R_{\mathcal{H}}\cup V_{\mathcal{G}}\times V_{\mathcal{H}})\\[4.30554pt]
{\mathcal{G}\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}\mathcal{H}}&=&(V_{\mathcal{G}}\cup V_{\mathcal{H}},\;R_{\mathcal{G}}\cup R_{\mathcal{H}}\cup(V_{\mathcal{G}}\times V_{\mathcal{H}})\cup(V_{\mathcal{H}}\times V_{\mathcal{G}}))\end{array}

{defi}

The mapping
{\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket}
from
linear generalized
formulas over a
set
X
to digraphs with vertices in
X
is defined inductively as follows:

{\llbracket\mathord{\mathbb{I}}\rrbracket}=\varnothing\quad{\llbracket x\rrbracket}=\bullet_{x}\quad{\llbracket{A\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}B}\rrbracket}={{\llbracket A\rrbracket}\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\llbracket B\rrbracket}}\quad{\llbracket{A\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}B}\rrbracket}={{\llbracket A\rrbracket}\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\llbracket B\rrbracket}}\quad{\llbracket{A\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}B}\rrbracket}={{\llbracket A\rrbracket}\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\llbracket B\rrbracket}}

where
\varnothing
is an abbreviation for the empty graph

(\varnothing,\varnothing)
and
\bullet_{x}=(\{x\},\varnothing)
is the
unique digraph that has
x
as its single vertex. Note that linearity of formulas is
required to fulfill the disjointness assumption of the previous definition.

{defi}

If
A
is a generalized formula over a set
X
that is
not assumed to be
linear
, we may translate it to a digraph as follows. Choose a
linear

generalized formula
A^{\prime}
, a set
Y
and a map
\ell:Y\to X
such that
A

is obtained from
A^{\prime}
by the substitution induced by
\ell
. Then the labeled
digraph
({\llbracket A^{\prime}\rrbracket},\ell)
corresponds to
A
. We may write it
{\llbracket A\rrbracket}
, keeping in mind that it is only defined up to isomorphism of labeled
digraphs.

Proposition 6
.

For every linear generalized formula
A
we have
9
9

9

Recall that
the notation
\left|A\right|
has been introduced in Definition
2.1
.

\left|A\right|=\left|V_{\mathcal{{\llbracket A\rrbracket}}}\right|
.

{exa}

The last digraph in
Equation

1
is
{\llbracket{\langle{({b\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}a})\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}d}\rangle\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}c}\rrbracket}
.

The point of the map
{\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket}
is that it gives an intrinsic representation of (generalized)
formulas modulo
\equiv
, as the following property shows.

{thmC}

[
[
Gug07
, Theorem 2.2.9]
]
For any two generalized formulas
A
and
B
over some set
X
, we have

A\equiv B
if and only if
{\llbracket A\rrbracket}
and
{\llbracket B\rrbracket}
are isomorphic labeled
digraphs.
If
A
and
B
are linear, this can be stated as
A\equiv B\iff{\llbracket A\rrbracket}={\llbracket B\rrbracket}
.

In
[
Gug07
]
, this property is stated
in terms of
“structures”
and
“relation webs”
. The former are
just formulas modulo
\equiv
while the latter are digraphs defined through a
quadruple of relations as in Remark
5
. Further discussion of relation
webs will take place later in this subsection (Definition
2.2
).

An immediate consequence of Theorem
2.2
, combined with
Proposition
2
, is that we can also translate sequents into
labeled digraphs.

Corollary 7
.

The extension of the map
{\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket}
to sequents by

{\llbracket{[\Gamma,\Delta]}\rrbracket}={{\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket}\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\llbracket\Delta\rrbracket}}\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad{\llbracket{\langle\Gamma;\Delta\rangle}\rrbracket}={{\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket}\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\llbracket\Delta\rrbracket}}

is well-defined up to isomorphism of labeled digraphs. Furthermore, if the
formula
A
corresponds to the sequent
\Gamma
according to
Definition
8
, then
{\llbracket A\rrbracket}
and
{\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket}
are
isomorphic.

{exa}

The last digraph in
Equation

1
is also
{\llbracket{[{\langle{b\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}a};d\rangle},c]}\rrbracket}
.

The next interesting question is how we can characterize the graphs that are
translations of formulas or sequents. In fact, they form the class of

directed cographs
(which we shall abbreviate as
“dicograph”
as
in
[
Ret21
]
, cf. Def.
2.2
); we refer to

[
BG18
, Section 11.6]
for a survey. It admits three
characterizations that have been found independently
in
[
BdGR97
,
Gug07
,
CP06
]
. They all rely on the notion of induced
subgraph.

{defi}

Let
\mathcal{G}=(V_{\mathcal{G}},R_{\mathcal{G}})
be a digraph and let

V_{\mathcal{H}}\subseteq V_{\mathcal{G}}
. The
subdigraph
of
\mathcal{G}

induced by

V_{\mathcal{H}}
is
\mathcal{H}=(V_{\mathcal{H}},R_{\mathcal{H}})
where
R_{\mathcal{H}}=R_{\mathcal{G}}\cap(V_{\mathcal{H}}\times V_{\mathcal{H}})
.
In this case we also
say that
\mathcal{H}
is an
induced subgraph
of
\mathcal{G}
and denote
that by
\mathcal{H}\sqsubseteq\mathcal{G}
. If additionally
V_{\mathcal{H}}\subsetneq V_{\mathcal{G}}
then
we write
\mathcal{H}\sqsubset\mathcal{G}
.

We give detailed statements for the characterizations from
[
BdGR97
,
Gug07
]

below, for the sake of completeness, and to show that they are very similar.

{defi}

An undirected graph is
\mathbf{P_{4}}
-free
if it does not contain a
\mathbf{P_{4}}

(shown on the left below) as induced subgraph, and a directed graph is

\mathbf{N}
-free
if it does not contain an
\mathbf{N}
(shown on the right below)
as induced subgraph.

(2)

{defiC}

[
[
BdGR97
, Prop. 5.3]
]

A
dicograph
is a digraph
\mathcal{G}=(V_{\mathcal{G}},R_{\mathcal{G}})
such that

(1)

the undirected graph
(V_{\mathcal{G}},R^{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.36165pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}}_{\mathcal{G}})
is
\mathbf{P_{4}}
-free,

(2)

the directed graph
(V_{\mathcal{G}},R^{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.36165pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}}_{\mathcal{G}})
is
\mathbf{N}
-free, and

(3)

the relation
R_{\mathcal{G}}
is
weakly transitive
:

•

if
(u,v)\in R^{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.36165pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}}_{\mathcal{G}}
and
(v,w)\in R_{\mathcal{G}}
then
(u,w)\in R_{\mathcal{G}}
, and

•

if
(u,v)\in R_{\mathcal{G}}
and
(v,w)\in R^{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.36165pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}}_{\mathcal{G}}
then
(u,w)\in R_{\mathcal{G}}
.

{defiC}

[
[
Gug07
, Theorem 2.2.4]
]
A
relation web
is a tuple

(V,R^{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.36165pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}},R^{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.36165pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}},R^{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleright$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleright$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.36165pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleright$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleright$}}}},R^{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.36165pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}})
obeying the four
conditions in Remark
5
together with the following
conditions:

(5)

the relations
R^{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.36165pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}}
and
R^{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleright$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleright$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.36165pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleright$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}
are transitive,

(6)

Triangular property:
for any

R_{1},R_{2},R_{3}\in\{R^{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.36165pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}}\cup R^{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleright$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleright$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.36165pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleright$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleright$}}}},R^{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.36165pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}},R^{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.36165pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}}\}
,
it holds:

if
(u,v)\in R_{1}
and
(v,w)\in R_{2}
and
(w,u)\in R_{3}
then
R_{1}=R_{2}
or

R_{2}=R_{3}
or
R_{3}=R_{1}
,

(7)

Square property:

(V,R^{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.36165pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}})
and
(V,R^{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.36165pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}})
are

\mathbf{P_{4}}
-free, and
(V,R^{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.36165pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}})
is
\mathbf{N}
-free.

Theorem 8
.

Let
\mathcal{G}
be a digraph. Then the following are equivalent:

(1)

There is a linear generalized formula
A
with
\mathcal{G}={\llbracket A\rrbracket}
.

(2)

\mathcal{G}
is a dicograph.

(3)

(V_{\mathcal{G}},R^{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.36165pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}}_{\mathcal{G}},R^{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.36165pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}}_{\mathcal{G}},R^{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleright$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleright$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.36165pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleright$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}_{\mathcal{G}},R^{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.36165pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}}_{\mathcal{G}})
is a relation web.

The equivalence
1\iff 2
has been shown in
[
BdGR97
, Section 5]
, while
the equivalence
1\iff 3
can be found in
[
Gug07
, Theorems 2.2.4 and 2.2.7]
.
A direct proof of
2\iff 3
is a straightforward exercise, that we encourage our readers to do by themselves.

Let us also briefly mention the characterization of dicographs
from
[
CP06
]
, that uses forbidden induced subgraphs as its only
condition.

{thmC}

[
[
CP06
, Thm. 2]
]

There exists a set
F
of 8 isomorphism classes of digraphs such that the
class of digraphs whose induced subgraphs are not in
F
coincides with the
class inductively generated by the operations of
Definition
2.2
starting from single-vertex graphs.

It follows from the definitions that this inductively generated class
corresponds to the first item of Theorem
8
, so this is indeed a
characterization of dicographs.

Corollary 9
.

Let
\mathcal{G}=(V_{\mathcal{G}},R_{\mathcal{G}})
be a dicograph. Then any induced
subdigraph of
\mathcal{G}
is also a dicograph.

Proof 2.1
.

This is because the induced subgraph relation
\sqsubseteq
is transitive.

An important special case of relation webs is when
R^{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.36165pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}}=\varnothing
.

Proposition 10
.

Let
\mathcal{G}
be a digraph. The following are equivalent:

(1)

There is a linear generalized formula
A
that does not contain
\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}

and such that
\mathcal{G}={\llbracket A\rrbracket}
.

(2)

\mathcal{G}
is a dicograph and
R^{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.36165pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}}_{\mathcal{G}}=\varnothing
, i.e. all edges are
unidirectional.

Furthermore, when these conditions hold,
\mathcal{G}=(V_{\mathcal{\mathcal{G}}},R^{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.36165pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}}_{\mathcal{\mathcal{G}}})
. For a fixed
V_{\mathcal{\mathcal{G}}}
, the possible
values for
R^{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.36165pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}}_{\mathcal{\mathcal{G}}}
are exactly those for which
\mathcal{G}
is
\mathbf{N}
-free and

R^{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.36165pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}}_{\mathcal{\mathcal{G}}}
is transitive. Such relations are called
series-parallel
orders
10
10

10

Strictly speaking, series-parallel orders are a special
case of
partial orders
, i.e.
reflexive
and transitive
relations, whereas
R^{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.36165pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}}_{\mathcal{\mathcal{G}}}
is
irreflexive
and transitive. So
it is the reflexive closure of
R^{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.36165pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}}_{\mathcal{\mathcal{G}}}
which is a series-parallel
order, but we often harmlessly identify (reflexive) posets with the
corresponding (irreflexive) digraphs.
in the literature.

Proof 2.2
.

Let
A
be a linear generalized formula. By structural induction on
A
, one
can see that
R^{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.36165pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}}_{\mathcal{{\llbracket A\rrbracket}}}=\varnothing
if and only if
A
is

\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}
-free. The claimed equivalence then follows from the case of general
dicographs we saw earlier. And by definition,
R^{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.36165pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}}_{\mathcal{\mathcal{G}}}
contains all the
unidirectional edges of
\mathcal{G}
.
The characterization as
\mathbf{N}
-free partial orders is just the specialization
of either Definition
2.2
or Definition
2.2
, but it is in fact
an old classical result
[
VTL82
, Section 4]
(see also
[
Mö89
]
).

The operations
\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}
and
\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}
on digraphs (Definition
2.2
)
are respectively the
“series composition”
and
“parallel
composition”
of partial orders. For more on series-parallel orders, see the
beginning of
[
BG18
, Section 11.6]
which points to many further
references.

Remark 11
.

Combining Propositions
3

and
10
, we see that sequents are equivalent to
series-parallel posets labeled by formulas up to label-preserving isomorphism,
i.e. to series-parallel
partially ordered multisets (pomsets)
of
formulas, as claimed in the previous subsection.

Remark 12
.

Another noteworthy special case of dicographs is those with

R^{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.36165pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}}_{\mathcal{G}}=R^{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleright$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleright$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.36165pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleright$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}_{\mathcal{G}}=\varnothing
; they are the digraphs of the form

{\llbracket A\rrbracket}
where
A
does not contain
\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}
. They correspond to a class of

undirected
graphs called
cographs
, that can be defined
equivalently as
\mathbf{P_{4}}
-free graphs (see
[
CLB81
]
). Cographs therefore
provide a representation of
\mathord{\mathsf{MLL\!+\!mix}}
formulas that can be used to build proof
nets (analogously to the next two sections for pomset logic),
see
[
Ret99a
,
Ret03
]
.

The class of
“directed cographs”
has been given this name
independently by
[
Ret99b
]
and
[
CP06
]
. This arguably attests that
it is a natural generalization of cographs.

2.3.
Perfect Matchings, RB-digraphs and Alternating Elementary Cycles

We have just seen representation of
formulas
as graphs. In this
subsection and the next one, we recall how to graphically represent pomset logic

proofs
as well. This requires a few graph-theoretic definitions first.

Recall that an undirected graph is 1-regular if every vertex is incident to
exactly one edge, and a perfect matching of some undirected graph is a 1-regular
spanning subgraph (so the notion of perfect matching is usually defined
relatively to some ambient graph). As a slight abuse of language, we use

“perfect matching”
in this paper to designate a directed counterpart to
1-regular graphs.

{defi}

We say that a digraph
\mathcal{G}=(V_{\mathcal{G}},E_{\mathcal{G}})
is a
perfect matching
when

(1)

any vertex has exactly one outgoing edge in
E_{\mathcal{G}}
and exactly one
incoming edge in
E_{\mathcal{G}}
, i.e., for every
u\in V_{\mathcal{G}}
there is a
single pair
(v,w)\in V_{\mathcal{G}}^{2}
such that
(u,v)\in E_{\mathcal{G}}
and
(w,u)\in E_{\mathcal{G}}
,
and

(2)

all edges are bidirectional, i.e. for all
u,v\in V_{\mathcal{G}}
, we
have that
(u,v)\in E_{\mathcal{G}}
iff
(v,u)\in E_{\mathcal{G}}
. This means, in particular,
that in the previous item,
v=w
.

{defi}

An
RB-digraph

\mathcal{G}=(V_{\mathcal{G}},R_{\mathcal{G}},B_{\mathcal{G}})
is a triple where

(V_{\mathcal{G}},R_{\mathcal{G}})
is an arbitrary digraph and
(V_{\mathcal{G}},B_{\mathcal{G}})
is a perfect
matching. We call the edges in
B_{\mathcal{G}}
the
matching edges
or

B
-edges
, and those in
R_{\mathcal{G}}
the
non-matching edges
or

R
-edges
.
We say that
\mathcal{G}
is an
RB-dicograph
iff
(V_{\mathcal{G}},R_{\mathcal{G}})
is a dicograph.

Following Definition
2.2
, we define an
isomorphism
between

\mathcal{G}
and another RB-digraph
\mathcal{H}=(V_{\mathcal{H}},R_{\mathcal{H}},B_{\mathcal{H}})
to be a bijection
f:V_{\mathcal{G}}\xrightarrow{\;\sim\;}V_{\mathcal{H}}
such that
f(R_{\mathcal{G}})=R_{\mathcal{H}}
and
f(B_{\mathcal{G}})=B_{\mathcal{H}}
. This
extends in the only sensible way to a notion of isomorphism between labeled
RB-digraphs.

{nota}

In all figures representing RB-digraphs, we will
(following
[
Ret99b
]
) draw the matching edges

bold and blue
, while the non-matching edges will be drawn

regular and red
.

{exa}

Here are six examples of RB-digraphs.

In the first two, the underlying digraphs are not dicographs. But the other four examples are RB-dicographs.

{defi}

Let
\mathcal{G}=(V_{\mathcal{G}},E_{\mathcal{G}})
be a digraph and
n\in\mathbb{N}
with
n\geq 2
. An
elementary path
of length
n
in
\mathcal{G}
is a sequence of vertices

u_{0},\ldots,u_{n}\in V_{\mathcal{G}}

without repetitions
such that
(u_{i},u_{i+1})\in E_{\mathcal{G}}
for all
i\in\{0,\dots,n-1\}
(so the length counts the number of
edges, not of vertices). An
elementary cycle
is defined in the same way
except there is the single repetition
u_{n}=u_{0}
(so the length of an
elementary cycle is both its number of vertices and its number of edges).

An
alternating elementary path
(or
æ-path
) in an RB-digraph

(V_{\mathcal{G}},R_{\mathcal{G}},B_{\mathcal{G}})
is an elementary path
u_{0},\ldots,u_{n}
in
the digraph
(V_{\mathcal{G}},R_{\mathcal{G}}\cup B_{\mathcal{G}})
such that
11
11

11

One could be tempted to simplify
this definition by saying that for any two consecutive edges, one is in

R_{\mathcal{G}}
and the other in
B_{\mathcal{G}}
; the issue, however, is that
R_{\mathcal{G}}
and
B_{\mathcal{G}}

are not required to be disjoint.
:

•

either
(u_{i},u_{i+1})\in R_{\mathcal{G}}
when
i
is odd and
(u_{i},u_{i+1})\in B_{\mathcal{G}}
when
i
is even,

•

or
(u_{i},u_{i+1})\in R_{\mathcal{G}}
when
i
is even and
(u_{i},u_{i+1})\in B_{\mathcal{G}}

when
i
is odd.

An
alternating elementary cycle
(or
æ-cycle
) in

(V_{\mathcal{G}},R_{\mathcal{G}},B_{\mathcal{G}})
is an elementary cycle
of even length
in

(V_{\mathcal{G}},R_{\mathcal{G}}\cup B_{\mathcal{G}})
that satisfies the above alternation condition.
Morally, the parity condition ensures that the cycle also alternates between

(u_{n-1},u_{n})
and
(u_{1},u_{2})
, or equivalently that the
“change of
base points”

u^{\prime}_{i}=u_{i+k\,\textrm{mod}\,n}
sends æ-cycles to æ-cycles.

{exa}

The first and the fifth graph in Example
2.3
do not contain
an æ-cycle. In all other graphs in that example, the four vertices
form an æ-cycle.

The existence or non-existence of æ-cycles in RB-dicographs will play a
central role in this paper. We note in passing that in the classical theory of
matchings in
undirected
graphs, the absence of æ-cycles admits
alternative characterizations and entails deep structural properties, which have
been applied to
\mathord{\mathsf{MLL\!+\!mix}}
proof nets
[
Ret03
,
Ngu20
]
.

{defi}

Let
u_{0},\dots,u_{n-1},u_{n}=u_{0}
be an elementary cycle in
\mathcal{G}=(V_{\mathcal{G}},E_{\mathcal{G}})
. An edge
(v,w)\in E_{\mathcal{G}}
is a
chord
for this cycle when

v=u_{i}
and
w=u_{j}
for some
i,j\in\{0,\dots,n-1\}
such that
i\not\equiv j+1\!\mod n
and
j\not\equiv i+1\mod n
. That is, both vertices
v
and
w

occur in the cycle but the edges
(v,w)
and
(w,v)
are not part of the
cycle. A cycle is
chordless
if it does not admit any chord in
\mathcal{G}
.
A
chordless æ-cycle
in an RB-digraph
(V_{\mathcal{G}},R_{\mathcal{G}},B_{\mathcal{G}})
is an æ-cycle
which has no chord in the total digraph
(V_{\mathcal{G}},R_{\mathcal{G}}\cup B_{\mathcal{G}})
. Note that since

B_{\mathcal{G}}
is a perfect matching, if an æ-cycle admits a chord, then this chord
is necessarily in
R_{\mathcal{G}}\setminus B_{\mathcal{G}}
.

{exa}

In Example
2.3
, the æ-cycles in the second and sixth
graph admit chords. The æ-cycles in the third and fourth graph are
chordless.

2.4.
Pomset Logic, Proof Nets and Balanced Formulas

A proof in multiplicative linear logic (
\mathsf{MLL}
) is given by its
conclusion (a formula or a sequent) and an
axiom linking
. This
can be drawn as a graph, which consists of the formula tree (or
sequent forest) with additional edges representing the axiom links,
i.e., connecting those leaves of the tree which are matched by an
axiom in the proof. In order to distinguish the actual proofs in the
set of all such graphs, a so-called
correctness criterion
is
employed, and the graphical structures that obey this criterion are
called
proof nets
.

In
[
Ret97a
]
, Retoré generalized this idea from the
formulas/sequents of
\mathsf{MLL}
to those that we introduced
in Section
2.1
. There exist many different equivalent
correctness criteria for
\mathsf{MLL}
, and one of Retoré’s main achievements
was to figure out which criterion allows for a generalization to
include the seq connective
\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}
. There are in fact two such criteria, and
their versions for
\mathsf{MLL}
and
\mathord{\mathsf{MLL\!+\!mix}}
have been presented in
[
Ret03
]
. They
are both based on RB-graphs, and we are now going to give an exposition of their extension
to RB-digraphs, as presented in
[
Ret97a
,
Ret99b
,
Ret21
]
.

First, the notion of axiom linking is the same as for
\mathsf{MLL}
.

{defi}

A
pomset logic pre-proof
of a formula or a sequent is an involution

\ell
on its set of atom occurrences such that an atom is always mapped
to its dual. This
\ell
is also called an
axiom linking
.

{exa}

The sequent
{[{a^{\bot}\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}a^{\bot}},{a\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}a}]}
has two possible axiom linkings.

Throughout this paper, the class of formulas for which there is a single
possible choice of axiom linking will play an important role.

{defi}

A formula is
balanced
if every propositional variable that occurs in

A
occurs exactly once positively and exactly once negatively. (Equivalently,
a formula is balanced when it is
linear
and its set of occurring atoms
is closed under duality.) Using the correspondence of
Definition
8
, this extends to a notion of
balanced
sequent
.
A balanced formula
A

uniquely determines
an axiom linking on
A
,
that we denote by
\ell(A)
. Similarly, we write
\ell(\Gamma)
for
the unique axiom linking on a balanced sequent
\Gamma
.
12
12

12

Balanced formulas for classical logic are discussed in detail in
[
Str12
, Section 7]
.

As we have seen in the previous subsections, one can represent a formula as a
dicograph, where the atom occurrences are the vertices and the edges are
determined by the connectives. More traditionally, one can also associate a
syntax tree to a formula. Each of these two graphical representations has an
associated correctness criterion, based on æ-cycles in RB-digraphs. We now
present those two criteria, starting with dicographs.

{defi}

Let
\Gamma
be a sequent and
\ell
be an axiom linking for

\Gamma
. The
cographic RB-prenet
of

\Gamma
and
\ell
, denoted by
\rho(\Gamma,\ell)
, is the
RB-dicograph
\mathcal{G}=(V_{\mathcal{G}},E_{\mathcal{G}},B_{\mathcal{G}})
where

(V_{\mathcal{G}},E_{\mathcal{G}})={\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket}
, and we have
(x,y)\in B_{\mathcal{G}}

iff the atom occurrences in
\Gamma
that correspond to
x
and
y
are mapped
to each other by the axiom linking
\ell
.

{exa}

The sequent
{[{\langle a;a\rangle},{\langle a^{\bot};a^{\bot}\rangle}]}
admits two possible axiom linkings, and the two corresponding cographic RB-prenets are the forth and the fifth graph in Example
2.3
.

{defi}

If
A
is a balanced formula, we write
(\mkern-4.0mu\llbracket A\rrbracket\mkern-4.0mu)
for the cographic RB-prenet
\rho(A,\ell(A))
,
i.e.,
(\mkern-4.0mu\llbracket A\rrbracket\mkern-4.0mu)=(V_{\mathcal{A}},R_{\mathcal{A}},B_{\mathcal{A}})
, where
(V_{\mathcal{A}},R_{\mathcal{A}})={\llbracket A\rrbracket}

and
B_{\mathcal{A}}
is the matching associated to
\ell(A)
.

Proposition 13
.

Every RB-dicograph is isomorphic to some
(\mkern-4.0mu\llbracket A\rrbracket\mkern-4.0mu)
where
A
is a
balanced formula.

Proof 2.3
.

Let
\mathcal{G}=(V_{\mathcal{G}},R_{\mathcal{G}},B_{\mathcal{G}})
be given. We can label the vertices in
V_{\mathcal{G}}

with distinct atoms, such that two atoms are dual if and only if they are
matched by
B_{\mathcal{G}}
. Then
A
exists by Theorem
8
, since

(V_{\mathcal{G}},R_{\mathcal{G}})
is a dicograph.

{defi}

A cographic RB-prenet is
correct
if it does not contain any
chordless æ-cycle. A correct cographic RB-prenet is also called a

cographic RB-net
.

{defi}

A sequent
\Gamma
is
provable in pomset logic
if there is an
axiom linking
\ell
for
\Gamma
, such that

\rho(\Gamma,\ell)
is correct. In that case,
\rho(\Gamma,\ell)
(or simply
\ell
) is a

pomset logic proof
of
\Gamma
.

{exa}

The last two graphs in Example
2.3
are pomset logic proofs for the balanced formulas
{\langle{a\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}b}\rangle\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}\langle{a^{\bot}\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}b^{\bot}}\rangle}
and
{[{a\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}a^{\bot}}]\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}[{b\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}b^{\bot}}]}
, respectively.

The following theorem says that pomset logic is a conservative extension of
\mathord{\mathsf{MLL\!+\!mix}}
.

{thmC}

[
[
Ret03
, Theorem 7]
(see also Remark
12
)]
Let
\Gamma
be a flat sequent without any occurrence of
\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}
, and let
\ell
be an axiom linking for
\Gamma
. Then
\ell
is the axiom linking of an
\mathord{\mathsf{MLL\!+\!mix}}
sequent proof iff
\rho(\Gamma,\ell)
is correct.

The second correctness criterion, that we are going to show now, is more in the tradition of other
known correctness criteria for
\mathord{\mathsf{MLL\!+\!mix}}
, as it works on a structure that is
directly derived from the formula trees. More precisely, we define
inductively for each formula
C
its
RB-tree
, denoted as

\mathcal{T}_{\mathrm{RB}}(C)
, as shown in Figure
2
. Technically
speaking this not a tree in the graph-theoretical sense, but we use
the name as it carries the structure of the formula tree.

\mathcal{T}_{\mathrm{RB}}(\mathord{\mathbb{I}})

Figure 2
.
Inductive definition of RB-trees (which are not quite trees in the
sense of graph theory, though they resemble the syntax trees of formulas).
The root vertex is at the bottom.

If we have a sequent
\Gamma
, then
\mathcal{T}_{\mathrm{RB}}(\Gamma)
is obtained from
the RB-trees of the formulas in
\Gamma
which are connected at the roots
via the edges corresponding to the series-parallel order of the sequent
structure (see the discussion following Proposition
10
). In order to
obtain an RB-digraph, we need to add the

B
-edges corresponding to the linking
\ell
. We denote this
RB-digraph by
\tau(\Gamma,\ell)
and call it the

tree-like RB-prenet
of
\Gamma
and

\ell
.

{exa}

.

. The left one is a correct proof net, while the right one contains
