C=g^{\bot}
and

. We get the cycle

{({a\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}d})\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}\langle{a^{\bot}\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}d^{\bot}}\rangle}

{({e\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}h})\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}\langle{b\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}h^{\bot}}\rangle\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}\langle{e^{\bot}\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}b^{\bot}}\rangle}

{A\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}[{B\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}C}]}

{A\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}B}

{A\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}B}

{\langle{d\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}g}\rangle\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}\langle{g^{\bot}\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}d^{\bot}}\rangle}

A={\langle{e\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}f}\rangle\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}\langle{g\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}h}\rangle}

is symmetric to the
this one via the automorphism
\beta
. Otherwise, either
A
or

(or both) have the form
{x^{\bot}\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}y^{\bot}}
. It suffices to treat

in the

-rule, and

equal to its own conjugate up to the variable renaming performed by

on the
{x^{\bot}\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}y^{\bot}}
that

–

. We get the cycle
{a^{\bot}\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}a}

{h^{\bot}\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}h}

B={e^{\bot}\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}b^{\bot}}
. We get the cycle
{({e\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}h})\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}\langle{h^{\bot}\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}e^{\bot}}\rangle}
in the premise.

–

B={g^{\bot}\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}d^{\bot}}
. We get the cycle
{({a\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}d})\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}\langle{a^{\bot}\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}d^{\bot}}\rangle}
in the premise.

–

B={c^{\bot}\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}f^{\bot}}
. We get the cycle
{({f\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}h})\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}\langle{h^{\bot}\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}f^{\bot}}\rangle}
in the premise.

•

{[{A\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}C}]\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}B}

\scriptstyle\mathsf{\hat{s}_{3}}\;

{({A\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}B})\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}C}

: There are
two possibilities to match
{A\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}B}
: either with
{\langle{a\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}b}\rangle\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}\langle{c\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}d}\rangle}

or with
{\langle{e\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}f}\rangle\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}\langle{g\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}h}\rangle}
. Due to the commutativity of
\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}
, we have four possibilities to match
A
and
B
. Due to
the symmetries discussed above, we only need to consider the case
where
A={a\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}b}
and
B={c\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}d}
. There are now five cases how to match
C
:

–

C={\langle{e\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}f}\rangle\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}\langle{g\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}h}\rangle}
. We get the cycle
{({f\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}c})\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}\langle{c^{\bot}\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}f^{\bot}}\rangle}
in the premise.

–

C={a^{\bot}\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}h^{\bot}}
. We get the cycle
{({h^{\bot}\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}c})\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}\langle{c^{\bot}\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}f^{\bot}}\rangle\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}({f\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}h})}
in the premise.

–

C={e^{\bot}\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}b^{\bot}}
. We get the cycle
{({e^{\bot}\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}d})\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}\langle{g^{\bot}\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}d^{\bot}}\rangle\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}({e\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}g})}
in the premise.

–

C={g^{\bot}\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}d^{\bot}}
. We get the cycle
{d^{\bot}\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}d}
in the premise.

–

C={c^{\bot}\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}f^{\bot}}
. We get the cycle
{c^{\bot}\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}c}
in the premise.

•

{A\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}B}

\scriptstyle\mathsf{\hat{s}_{2}}\;

{A\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}B}

: This case is
already subsumed by the case for
\mathsf{\hat{q}_{2}}{\downarrow}
.

In this way, we have completed the proof of the following proposition.

Proposition 35
.

The formula
Q
shown in

Equation

10

is not provable in
{\mathsf{BV}}
.

Theorem 36
.

The theorems of
{\mathsf{BV}}
form a proper subset of the theorems of pomset logic.

Proof 3.9
.

This follows immediately from Propositions
34
and

.

Remark 37
.

Let us end this section by some explanation of how the formula
Q
has
been found. Our starting point was the so-called medial rule from
system
\mathsf{SKS}

[
BT01
]
, a formulation of classical
logic in the calculus of structures:

{({A\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}}D})\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\vee$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\vee$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}}({B\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}}C})}

\scriptstyle\mathsf{m}\;

{[{A\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\vee$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\vee$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}}B}]\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}}[{D\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\vee$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\vee$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}}C}]}

(12)

It corresponds to the classical sequent
{({A\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}}D})\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\vee$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\vee$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}}({B\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}}C})}\vdash{[{A\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\vee$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\vee$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}}B}]\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}}[{D\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\vee$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\vee$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}}C}]}
, which can be rewritten by De Morgan’s laws as

\vdash{[{\lnot A\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\vee$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\vee$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}}\lnot D}]\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}}[{\lnot B\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\vee$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\vee$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}}\lnot C}]},{[{A\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\vee$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\vee$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}}B}]\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\wedge$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\wedge$}}}[{D\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\vee$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\vee$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vee$}}}C}]}
.
By replacing the classical connectives and instantiating the formulas

A,B,C,D
with distinct atoms, we get the sequent
{[(a^{\bot}\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}d^{\bot})\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}(b^{\bot}\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}c^{\bot}),\,(a\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}b)\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}(d\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}c)]}
which is not
provable in linear logic.
This can be immediately seen by inspecting its RB-prenet,
shown in
Figure

10(a)
, which contains several (chordless)
æ-cycles.

Then, on the right of that
“medial RB-prenet”
, in

Figure

10(b)
, we replace the
B
-edges corresponding to the
atoms by a pair of
B
-edges connected by an (undirected)

R
-edge. This does not affect provability, as no æ-cycles are
added or removed. Then, in
Figure

10(c)
, we give
these new
R
-edges a direction. By choosing the right direction, we
can break all æ-cycles, which means the result becomes correct
with respect to the pomset logic correctness criterion. But the
resulting formula (or sequent) remains unprovable in
{\mathsf{BV}}
. To
simplify the proof of non-provability in
{\mathsf{BV}}
, we added further

R
-edges, as shown in
Figure

10(d)
,
that do not break provability in pomset logic. It is easy to see
that the RB-prenet in
Figure

10(d)
is
an intermediate step between the one in

Figure

7
and the one on the right in

Figure

8
.

In Example
4.3
we shall see that

Figure

10(c)
is also related to a construction that we use
for complexity-theoretic purposes; more than that, we shall explain how
complexity considerations allowed us to restrict the search space for a
formula separating pomset logic from
{\mathsf{BV}}
.

4.
Complexity of Provability

After having established that
{\mathsf{BV}}
and pomset logic are not the
same, the next natural question is whether they have the same or
different provability complexity. It had already been established
before that
{\mathsf{BV}}
is
\mathbf{NP}
-complete. We recall the proof here and
establish a slightly more general result. Then we discuss the
complexity of pomset logic and show that already checking the
correctness of a pomset logic proofnet is
\mathbf{coNP}
-complete. Based on
this observation, we can then show that provability in pomset logic is

\mathbf{\Sigma_{2}^{p}}
-complete.

4.1.
Preliminaries on Complexity Theory and Boolean Formulas

We assume that the reader is familiar with (see for instance the reference
textbook
[
AB09
, Chapter 2]
)

•

the complexity classes
\mathbf{P}
,
\mathbf{NP}
and
\mathbf{coNP}
;

•

the notions, for a complexity class
\mathcal{C}
, of
\mathcal{C}
-hard
and
\mathcal{C}
-complete problems (defined, as usual, with respect to
many-to-one polynomial time reductions for all
\mathcal{C}
that we shall
consider – though weaker reductions should also work).

\mathbf{NP}
and
\mathbf{coNP}
form the first level
of the
polynomial hierarchy

[
AB09
, Chapter 5]
, and we shall
also be concerned with its
second level
which contains the classes

\mathbf{\Sigma_{2}^{p}}
and
\mathbf{\Pi_{2}^{p}}
. These are dual in the same way that
\mathbf{NP}
and

\mathbf{coNP}
are: a decision problem is in
\mathbf{\Pi_{2}^{p}}
if and only if its negation is
in
\mathbf{\Sigma_{2}^{p}}
.

To show that a problem is in
\mathbf{\Sigma_{2}^{p}}
, the most convenient way is perhaps to
use the definition in terms of oracle machines.

{defiC}

[
[
AB09
, Section 5.5]
]

\mathbf{\Sigma_{2}^{p}}
is
\mathbf{NP}
extended with an
\mathbf{NP}
oracle; this is usually written
as
\mathbf{\Sigma_{2}^{p}}=\mathbf{NP}^{\mathbf{NP}}
. In other words, a decision problem is in

\mathbf{\Sigma_{2}^{p}}
if and only if it can be solved by an
\mathbf{NP}
algorithm that can
call constant time subroutines for problems in
\mathbf{NP}
.
Similarly,
\mathbf{\Pi_{2}^{p}}
is
\mathbf{coNP}
extended with an
\mathbf{NP}
oracle:
\mathbf{\Pi_{2}^{p}}=\mathbf{coNP}^{\mathbf{NP}}
.

Conversely, to show hardness results, we use complete problems involving

Boolean formulas
.
We consider a fixed set of
(Boolean) variables
. A
literal
is
either
x
or
\lnot x
for some variable
x
; a
clause
is a finite set
of literals; a
conjunctive normal form (CNF)
is a finite set of clauses.
The idea is that a CNF represents a Boolean formula, as in the following
example:

\{\{x,y,z\},\{\lnot x,y\},\{\lnot y,\lnot z\}\}\quad\rightsquigarrow\quad(x\lor y\lor z)\land(\lnot x\lor y)\land(\lnot y\lor\lnot z)

Consistent with this interpretation, a clause is said to be
satisfied

by some assignment from variables to Booleans in
\{\mathtt{true},\mathtt{false}\}
if it
contains
some
literal
l
such that for some variable
x
, either
l=x

and
x
is set to
\mathtt{true}
, or
l=\lnot x
and
x
is set to
\mathtt{false}
; and an
assignment is said to
satisfy
a CNF when it satisfies
all
its
clauses. The celebrated
Cook–Levin theorem
states that finding a
satisfying assignment for a CNF is
\mathbf{NP}
-complete. We recall its generalization
to the first two levels of the polynomial hierarchy.

{defi}

The problem
cnf-sat
consists in deciding, given a CNF as input, whether it
admits a satisfying assignment.
It is generalized by
\forall\exists
-cnf-sat
, which takes as input

•

a finite set of
universal variables

X=\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\}
,

•

a finite set of
existential variables

Y=\{y_{1},\ldots,y_{m}\}
,
disjoint from
X
,

•

and a CNF whose variables are included in
X\cup Y
,

the question being whether
every
partial assignment
X\to\{\mathtt{true},\mathtt{false}\}
can be extended to
some
assignment
X\cup Y\to\{\mathtt{true},\mathtt{false}\}
that satisfies the input CNF.

{thmC}

[
[
Wra76
, Cor. 6]
]

cnf-sat
is
\mathbf{NP}
-complete and
\forall\exists
-cnf-sat
is
\mathbf{\Pi_{2}^{p}}
-complete.

As an example, the above-mentioned CNF
\{\{x,y,z\},\{\lnot x,y\},\{\lnot y,\lnot z\}\}
, with the universal variables
\{x,y\}
and the existential
variable
\{z\}
, is a negative instance of
\forall\exists
-cnf-sat
since the
corresponding quantified Boolean formula

\forall x\;\forall y\;\exists z.\;(x\lor y\lor z)\land(\lnot x\lor y)\land(\lnot y\lor\lnot z)

(where the quantifiers range over
\{\mathtt{true},\mathtt{false}\}
) is false: for
x=\mathtt{true}

and
y=\mathtt{false}
, there is no choice of
z
that satisfies the clause
\lnot x\lor y
.
Let us conclude these preliminaries by mentioning that
\mathbf{coNP}
and
\mathbf{\Sigma_{2}^{p}}

admit complete problems involving formulas in
disjunctive
normal forms.

Remark 38
.

There is a standard reduction from
cnf-sat
to the case where
all
variables occur at least once positively and at least once negatively
,
which goes as follows. You can detect in polynomial time for which variables
this is not the case, e.g.
x
has only positive occurrences and
y
has only
negative occurrences. Then if you delete all clauses in which either
x
or

\lnot y
appear (or both), this does not change whether the set of clauses is
satisfiable (because if you set
x=\mathtt{true}
and
y=\mathtt{false}
you satisfy all
deleted clauses and do not lose any degree of freedom for the remaining
clauses). After this deletion maybe some other variable occurs with only one
polarity, so you need to iterate that procedure. But as the number of iterations
is bounded by the number of variables, this reduction is polynomial time.

Thus, this restriction of
cnf-sat
is
\mathbf{NP}
-complete. For similar reasons,
the instances of
\forall\exists
-cnf-sat
in which no atom appears with a single
polarity are also
\mathbf{\Pi_{2}^{p}}
-complete.

4.2.
{\mathsf{BV}}
is
\mathbf{NP}
-Complete (and how to Generalize Membership in
\mathbf{NP}
)

Let us first recall that the complexity of proof search in
{\mathsf{BV}}
is already
known.

Theorem 39

(Kahramanoğulları
[
Kah08
]
)
.

Provability in system
{\mathsf{BV}}
is
\mathbf{NP}
-complete.

The
\mathbf{NP}
-hardness part already applies to

\mathord{\mathsf{MLL\!+\!mix}}

[
Kah08
, Corollary 4.5]
,
which
{\mathsf{BV}}

conservatively extends; we shall not dwell on this here. We will merely remark
that while Kahramanoğulları proves that provability for
\mathord{\mathsf{MLL\!+\!mix}}
is
\mathbf{NP}
-hard using a syntactic
analysis of the calculus of structures, one could presumably adapt the more
traditional
phase semantics
methods used to study the hardness of other
variants of linear logic in order to get an alternative proof.

Membership in
\mathbf{NP}
is more interesting for us, since it demonstrates a
complexity gap with the
\mathbf{\Sigma_{2}^{p}}
-complete pomset logic (unless
\mathbf{NP}=\mathbf{coNP}
), as we already said. We recall here the proof that provability in
\mathsf{BVu}

is in
\mathbf{NP}
(the unit-free version is slightly more convenient), giving more
details than
[
Kah08
]
.

The first main argument is a bound on the length of proofs, relying on the
following property.

{defi}

We say that a rewriting system
\rightsquigarrow
on unit-free formulas is

dicograph-monotone
when
\mathrel{\equiv^{\prime}\mkern-2.0mu}\;\subseteq\;\rightsquigarrow
and for any

A\rightsquigarrow B
such that
A\not\mathrel{\equiv^{\prime}\mkern-2.0mu}B
:

•

either
\dfrac{B}{A}
is an instance of an interaction rule of
\mathsf{BVu}
(i.e., one of
\mathsf{ai^{\circ}\mkern-2.0mu}{\downarrow}
,
\mathsf{ai^{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.62221pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.62221pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.3733pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}}\mkern-3.0mu}{\downarrow}
,
\mathsf{ai^{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.62221pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.62221pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.3733pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}}_{L}\mkern-2.0mu}{\downarrow}
,
\mathsf{ai^{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.62221pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.62221pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.3733pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}}_{R}\mkern-2.0mu}{\downarrow}
);

•

or
R_{{\llbracket A\rrbracket}}\subsetneq R_{{\llbracket B\rrbracket}}
for a
suitable identification of atom occurrences of
A
and
B
ensuring that we
can consider the vertex sets
V_{{\llbracket A\rrbracket}}
and

V_{{\llbracket B\rrbracket}}
to be equal.

Proposition 40
.

A\rightsquigarrow B\iff\dfrac{B}{A}
in
\mathsf{BVu}
defines a
dicograph-monotone rewriting system.

Proof 4.1
.

This is a direct consequence of the easy direction (
“if”
) of
Theorem
2.7
.

As discussed in
Section

2.7
, the rules of
{\mathsf{SBV}}
are
originally derived from a characterization of dicograph inclusion.
Dicograph-monotonicity is therefore a fundamental aspect of the design of

{\mathsf{SBV}}
,
{\mathsf{BV}}
and their variants. We will discuss another example of this notion in
Remark
45
.

Proposition 41
.

Let
\rightsquigarrow
be a dicograph-monotone relation and

\rightsquigarrow^{*}
be its transitive closure. Then, for any
A\rightsquigarrow^{*}B
, there exists a path
A=C_{1}\rightsquigarrow\dots\rightsquigarrow C_{k}=B
with length
k=O(|A|^{2})
.

Proof 4.2
.

Since
\mathrel{\equiv^{\prime}\mkern-2.0mu}
is transitive by definition, if
C\rightsquigarrow D\rightsquigarrow E
with
C\mathrel{\equiv^{\prime}\mkern-2.0mu}D\mathrel{\equiv^{\prime}\mkern-2.0mu}E
, then
C\rightsquigarrow E

directly (by the assumption
\mathrel{\equiv^{\prime}\mkern-2.0mu}\;\subseteq\;\rightsquigarrow
). Thus,
in the path of minimum length between
A
and
B
, at least half of the
rewrites
C_{i}\rightsquigarrow C_{i+1}
satisfy
C_{i}\not\mathrel{\equiv^{\prime}\mkern-2.0mu}C_{i+1}
.
Therefore, up to a factor of two (that gets absorbed in the
O(\cdot)

notation), it suffices to bound the number of rewrites with
C_{i}\not\mathrel{\equiv^{\prime}\mkern-2.0mu}C_{i+1}
.

The definition of dicograph-monotonicity then gives us two cases. We claim
that in both cases,
V_{{\llbracket C_{i}\rrbracket}}^{2}\setminus R_{{\llbracket C_{i}\rrbracket}}\supsetneq V_{{\llbracket C_{i+1}\rrbracket}}^{2}\setminus R_{{\llbracket C_{i+1}\rrbracket}}
. First, if
V_{{\llbracket C_{i}\rrbracket}}=V_{{\llbracket C_{i+1}\rrbracket}}
and
R_{{\llbracket C_{i}\rrbracket}}\subsetneq R_{{\llbracket C_{i+1}\rrbracket}}
, then this is immediate. Otherwise,
C_{i}
is
inferred from
C_{i+1}
by an interaction rule, and then
{\llbracket C_{i+1}\rrbracket}

can be identified with an induced subgraph of
{\llbracket C_{i}\rrbracket}
with two fewer
vertices, resulting in a strict inclusion between the complement graphs as we
wanted.

Therefore, the natural number
|V_{{\llbracket C_{i}\rrbracket}}^{2}\setminus R_{{\llbracket C_{i}\rrbracket}}|
strictly decreases at each rewriting step such that

C_{i}\not\mathrel{\equiv^{\prime}\mkern-2.0mu}C_{i+1}
. So, starting from
A
, the number of such
steps is bounded by
|V_{{\llbracket A\rrbracket}}^{2}\setminus R_{{\llbracket A\rrbracket}}|=O(|A|^{2})
. (We see that our definition of
dicograph-monotonicity is slightly stronger than what we truly need from the
point of view of complexity.)

From this proposition, we immediately get:

Corollary 42
.

Any provable formula
A
in
\mathsf{BVu}
has a proof of length
O(|A|^{2})
& size
O(|A|^{3})
.

Here
“size”
refers to the complexity-theoretic notion of the number of
bits it takes to write out the proof, hence the additional factor of
|A|

accounting for the size of each intermediate formula in the derivation.
Now that we know that we have short proofs, it remains to show that they can be
checked efficiently.

Proposition 43
.

The validity of a proof in
\mathsf{BVu}
can be checked in
polynomial time
. In
other words, (our presentation of)
\mathsf{BVu}
is a
Cook–Reckhow proof
system
[
CR79
]
.

Proof 4.3
.

It suffices to show that the validity of each inference rule can be verified
in polynomial time.

For instances of the
\mathrel{\equiv^{\prime}\mkern-2.0mu}
rule, to check that
A\mathrel{\equiv^{\prime}\mkern-2.0mu}B
, one can apply
a generic recipe for terms over associative and possibly commutative binary
operators: compute hereditarily flattened and possibly sorted representations
of
A
and
B
in polynomial time, and compare them, as sketched in the
introduction of
[
Bas94
]
for example.

Let us now consider any other rule
\mathsf{r}
of
\mathsf{BVu}
. There exist two formulas

A_{\mathsf{r}}
and
B_{\mathsf{r}}
such that the instances of these rules are precisely the
inferences of the form

\frac{S\{A_{\mathsf{r}}[a_{1}:=C_{1},\dots,a_{k}:=C_{k}]\}}{S\{B_{\mathsf{r}}[a_{1}:=C_{1},\dots,a_{k}:=C_{k}]\}}

where
S\{\cdot\}
is a context,
\{a_{1},\dots,a_{k}\}
is the set of
propositional variables that appear in either
A
or
B
(often both), and

[a_{1}:=C_{1},\dots,a_{k}:=C_{k}]
denotes a parallel substitution of those
variables by formulas. For instance, for
\mathsf{r}=\mathsf{q_{2}}{\downarrow}
, we may take
k=2
,

A_{\mathsf{q_{2}}{\downarrow}}={a_{1}\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}a_{2}}
and
B_{\mathsf{q_{2}}{\downarrow}}={a_{1}\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}a_{2}}
.

Given two formulas
A
and
B
, our task is to decide in polynomial time
whether one may infer
B
from
A
using the rule
\mathsf{r}
. To do so, we first
enumerate in polynomial time all the pairs
(S\{\cdot\},A^{\prime})
such that

S\{A^{\prime}\}=A
(there are
O(|A|)
many). For each of these pairs, we match

A^{\prime}
against the pattern
A_{\mathsf{r}}
in linear time; if this succeeds, we get a
substitution such that
A_{\mathsf{r}}[a_{1}:=C_{1},\dots,a_{k}:=C_{k}]=A^{\prime}
. To
conclude, we just have to test the equality
S\{B_{\mathsf{r}}[a_{1}:=C_{1},\dots,a_{k}:=C_{k}]\}=B
.

Remark 44
.

In this proof, we have exploited the fact that the
\mathrel{\equiv^{\prime}\mkern-2.0mu}
rules appear
explicitly in our formal proofs. This differs from some traditional
presentations of logics in the calculus of structures, which consider that the
inference rules work over equivalence classes of formulas modulo
\equiv
or

\mathrel{\equiv^{\prime}\mkern-2.0mu}
(those classes are called
structures
in
[
Gug07
]
). In such a presentation with
\mathrel{\equiv^{\prime}\mkern-2.0mu}
kept implicit, it is less obvious that proofs are still polynomial-time checkable.

Together, Proposition
42
and Proposition
43
tell us
that
\mathsf{BVu}
has polynomially bounded proofs that can be checked in polynomial
time. This entails that provability is in
\mathbf{NP}
. To extend the result from

\mathsf{BVu}
to
{\mathsf{BV}}
, note that for any formula
A
with units, there is a unit-free
formula
A^{\prime}
such that
A\equiv A^{\prime}
, which is computable from
A
in polynomial
time, and then
\vdash_{{\mathsf{BV}}}A\iff\vdash_{\mathsf{BVu}}A^{\prime}
.

Remark 45
.

Let us revisit Tiu’s result
[
Tiu06
]
on the incompleteness of

“shallow systems”
with respect to
{\mathsf{BV}}
in the light of our complexity
results. The main difference between shallow and deep inference is the lack of
contextual closure of the rules in the former, i.e.

{\vbox{\hbox{\kern 5.16939pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{$B$}}}}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{r}\;$}}$}}\vbox{\vbox to0.4pt{\vfill\hbox to8.0868pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt}\vfill}}\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{$$}}$}\hss}\kern 0.0pt}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\kern 0.2934pt\hbox{\hbox{$\kern 1.25pt\hbox{$A$}\kern 1.25pt$}}\kern 0.2934pt}}}\kern 0.0pt}}}\;\;\;\not\!\!\!\implies{\vbox{\hbox{\kern 5.16939pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{$S\{B\}$}}}}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathsf{r}\;$}}$}}\vbox{\vbox to0.4pt{\vfill\hbox to24.79515pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt}\vfill}}\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{$$}}$}\hss}\kern 0.0pt}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\kern 0.2934pt\hbox{\hbox{$\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$S\{A\}$}\kern 0.0pt$}}\kern 0.2934pt}}}\kern 0.0pt}}}

One interpretation put forth for the results of
[
Tiu06
]
is that proof
systems that can be translated into shallow systems, such as traditional
sequent calculi, fail to capture
{\mathsf{BV}}

and pomset
logic
15
15

15

From
[
Tiu06
, §1]
:
“We conjecture that [system

{\mathsf{BV}}
and pomset logic] are actually the same logic. The result on the
necessity of deep-inference of
{\mathsf{BV}}
therefore explains to some extent the
difficulty in the sequentialization of Pomset logic.”
because of the lack
of deep inference.

We claim that in the case of pomset logic, there is an obstruction unrelated
to the shallow vs deep distinction. Indeed, the definition for shallow systems
in
[
Tiu06
, §6]
also enforces the condition that we called
dicograph-monotonicity: observe that the relation
A\prec B
given in

[
Tiu06
, Definition 6.1]
is equivalent to
R_{{\llbracket A\rrbracket}}\subset R_{{\llbracket B\rrbracket}}
. (Being able to state this is one reason for talking
abstractly about rewriting systems instead of working directly in the calculus
of structures.)

Therefore, by Proposition
41
, any shallow system in this
sense has polynomially bounded proofs; and if those proofs are also
polynomial-time checkable
then provability is in
\mathbf{NP}
. Together with the result of the next section, this shows that these systems cannot capture
pomset logic unless
\mathbf{NP}=\mathbf{coNP}
.

4.3.
Correctness of Pomset Logic Proof Nets is
\mathbf{coNP}
-Complete

As an intermediate step towards our eventual hardness result for provability in
pomset logic, we first study the
correctness
problem: given a prenet
(either tree-like or cographic), does it satisfy the correctness criterion,
that is, is it an actual proof
net? This can be seen as the special case of provability for
balanced

formulas, as we remarked before. Let us state our result right away.

Theorem 46
.

The correctness problem for pomset logic proof nets is
\mathbf{coNP}
-complete.
More precisely, given a sequent
\Gamma
and a pre-proof (or linking)

\ell
, it is
\mathbf{coNP}
-complete to check the correctness of its cographic
RB-prenet
\rho(\Gamma,\ell)
or of its tree-like RB-prenet

\tau(\Gamma,\ell)
(those two conditions being equivalent by
Theorem
2.4
).

Proof 4.4
.

Let us show an equivalent reformulation: it is
\mathbf{NP}
-complete to decide
whether a pre-proof is
incorrect
. Membership in
\mathbf{NP}
is immediate: one
can build
\tau(\Gamma,\ell)
, whose æ-cycles provide witnesses for
incorrectness by definition, in polynomial time; the size of those cycles is
bounded by the number of vertices, and they can be checked in polynomial time.
As for the proof of
\mathbf{NP}
-hardness, it is done in two steps.

•

We first show, via a polynomial time reduction, that incorrectness is as
hard as finding æ-cycles in
arbitrary
RB-digraphs
(Proposition
48
and Theorem
49
).

•

We then prove in the next subsection that the existence of æ-cycles
for general RB-digraphs is
\mathbf{NP}
-hard (Theorem
50
).

Remark 47
.

Assuming that
\mathbf{P}\neq\mathbf{NP}
, this refutes

[
Ret97a
, Prop. 5]
which claims that a
“standard breadth
search algorithm”
can decide pomset proof net correctness in polynomial
time. (The issue with this kind of argument is discussed
in
[
Ngu20
, §8.1]
.)
This claim was meant to justify that
“the proof net
syntax is a sensible syntax by itself”

[
Ret97a
, §3]
: it is
indeed part of the Cook–Reckhow definition of proof
system
[
CR79
]
, as already mentioned in
Proposition
43
, which says that the
verification of
{\mathsf{BV}}
derivations is in
\mathbf{P}
.

We are now going to fill the steps outlined above to prove the

\mathbf{NP}
-hardness part of Theorem
46
. The reduction step
extends the
“proofification”
construction
from
[
Ngu20
]
that sends
undirected
perfect
matchings to
\mathord{\mathsf{MLL\!+\!mix}}
pre-proof nets. For the sake of clarity, we
present our new reduction, with the same name, as a map from
arbitrary RB-digraphs to balanced pomset logic sequents. Since the
procedure makes arbitrary choices, the result is defined only up to
atom renaming and modulo
\equiv
, but correctness and provability are
indeed invariant for these equivalences.

The following definition (which is illustrated by
Figure

11
) makes use of the notation
R^{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.36165pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}}_{\mathcal{\mathcal{G}}},R^{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.36165pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}}_{\mathcal{\mathcal{G}}},R^{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleright$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleright$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.36165pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleright$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}_{\mathcal{\mathcal{G}}},R^{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.36165pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}}_{\mathcal{\mathcal{G}}}
introduced in Definition
2.2
.

{defi}

Let
\mathcal{G}=(V_{\mathcal{G}},R_{\mathcal{G}},B_{\mathcal{G}})
be an RB-digraph.
The
proofification
of

\mathcal{G}
, denoted by
\Pi(\mathcal{G})
, is the balanced (flat) sequent (defined only modulo
\equiv
and variable renaming)

{[{C_{u_{1}}\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}C_{v_{1}}},\dots,{C_{u_{m}}\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}C_{v_{m}}},D_{e_{1}},\dots,D_{e_{k}}]}

where:

•

\{(u_{1},v_{1}),(v_{1},u_{1}),\dots,(u_{m},v_{m}),(v_{m},u_{m})\}=B_{\mathcal{G}}
and

\{e_{1},\dots,e_{k}\}=R^{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.36165pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}}_{\mathcal{\mathcal{G}}}
, both of these being non-repeating
enumerations;

•

for

, we let
C_{u}={a_{u,w_{1}}\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}\dots\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}a_{u,w_{n}}}
where

w_{1},\ldots,w_{n}
is a non-repeating enumeration of the neighborhood of
u
,
i.e.
\{(u,w_{1}),\dots,(u,w_{n})\}=(\{u\}\times V_{\mathcal{G}})\cap(R^{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.36165pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}}_{\mathcal{\mathcal{G}}}\cup R^{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.36165pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}}_{\mathcal{\mathcal{G}}}\cup R^{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleright$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleright$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.36165pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleright$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}_{\mathcal{\mathcal{G}}})
, and

–

for each
\{u,v\}\subseteq V_{\mathcal{G}}
such that
(u,v)\in R^{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.36165pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}}_{\mathcal{\mathcal{G}}}

(equivalently,
(v,u)\in R^{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.36165pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}}_{\mathcal{\mathcal{G}}}
), we give the names
a_{u,v}
and

a_{v,u}
to a fresh pair of
dual
atoms;

–

for each
(u,v)\in R^{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.36165pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}}_{\mathcal{\mathcal{G}}}
(equivalently,
(v,u)\in R^{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleright$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleright$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.36165pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleright$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}_{\mathcal{\mathcal{G}}}
), we generate two fresh atoms
a_{u,v}
and
a_{v,u}
that
are
not
dual;

•

for
(u,v)\in R^{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.36165pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}}_{\mathcal{\mathcal{G}}}
, we let
D_{(u,v)}={a_{u,v}^{\bot}\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}a_{v,u}^{\bot}}
, using the above-defined atoms.

The ideas that were already present in
[
Ngu20
, Section 3.2]
are the use of
par

(
\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}
) to represent vertices,
tensor
(
\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}
) for matching edges,
and dual atoms for non-matching edges. The novelty here is how the

(
\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}
) connective of pomset logic serves to encode edge directions through
the formulas
D_{(u,v)}
. It is immediate from the definition that:

w
x
y
z
a_{w,x}
a_{x,w}
a_{x,y}
a_{x,y}^{\bot}
a_{y,x}
a_{y,x}^{\bot}
a_{y,z}
a_{y,z}^{\bot}
a_{z,y}
a_{z,y}^{\bot}

{[~{a_{w,x}\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}[{a_{y,z}\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}a_{y,x}}]}~,~{[{a_{x,y}\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}a_{x,w}}]\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}a_{z,y}}~,~{a_{y,x}^{\bot}\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}a_{x,y}^{\bot}}~,~{a_{y,x}^{\bot}\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}a_{z,y}^{\bot}}~]}

Figure 11
.
An RB-digraph (left) and its proofification (below) together with the corresponding cographic RB-prenet (right). Note that
a_{w,x}
and
a_{x,w}
are defined to be

Proposition 48
.

Proofifications can be computed in polynomial time.

Since a balanced sequent (or formula) uniquely defines a (tree-like or cographic) prenet, we can define the
correctness
of such a sequent (or formula), to be the correctness of the corresponding prenet.

Theorem 49
.

Let
\mathcal{G}
be an RB-digraph and
\Pi(\mathcal{G})
be its proofification. Then

\mathcal{G}
admits an æ-cycle if and only if
\Pi(\mathcal{G})

is incorrect.

Proof 4.5
.

By slightly adapting the reasoning found
in
[
Ngu20
, Proposition 3.9]
, one could show that the the æ-cycles in

\mathcal{G}
are in bijection with those in the tree-like RB-prenet of

\Pi(\mathcal{G})
, and the theorem statement would then follow immediately.
However, we find it more convenient here to work with the cographic RB-prenet

(\mkern-4.0mu\llbracket\Pi(\mathcal{G})\rrbracket\mkern-4.0mu)=\rho(\Pi(\mathcal{G}),\ell(\Pi(\mathcal{G})))

(see
Figure

11
for an example).

First, by unfolding the definitions, one can write the set of non-matching
edges of
(\mkern-4.0mu\llbracket\Pi(\mathcal{G})\rrbracket\mkern-4.0mu)
as the disjoint union
R_{1}\cup R_{2}

where

\displaystyle R_{1}

\displaystyle=\{(a_{u,v},a_{x,y})\mid(u,x)\in B_{\mathcal{G}}\land(u,v),(x,y)\in R^{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.36165pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}}_{\mathcal{\mathcal{G}}}\cup R^{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.36165pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}}_{\mathcal{\mathcal{G}}}\cup R^{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleright$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleright$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.36165pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleright$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleright$}}}}_{\mathcal{\mathcal{G}}}\}

\displaystyle R_{2}

\displaystyle=\{(a_{u,v}^{\bot},a_{v,u}^{\bot})\mid(u,v)\in R_{\mathcal{G}}\land(v,u)\notin R_{\mathcal{G}}\}

Next, suppose that there exists an æ-cycle
u_{0},\dots,u_{n-1},u_{n}=u_{0}
in

\mathcal{G}
, assuming without loss of generality that
(u_{i},u_{i+1})\in B_{\mathcal{G}}
when

i
is even and
(u_{i},u_{i+1})\in R_{\mathcal{G}}
when
i
is odd. Let us write
a[u,v]=a_{u,v}
for readability. The aforementioned æ-cycle can be turned into the
following cycle in
(\mkern-4.0mu\llbracket\Pi(\mathcal{G})\rrbracket\mkern-4.0mu)
, where
\to
denotes a
non-matching edge and
\Rightarrow
denotes either a matching edge or an æ-path of
length 3 starting and ending with matching edges:

a[u_{0},u_{n-1}]\to a[u_{1},u_{2}]\Rightarrow a[u_{2},u_{1}]\to a[u_{3},u_{4}]\Rightarrow\dots\to a[u_{n-1},u_{0}]\Rightarrow a[u_{0},u_{n-1}]

The fact that it is an æ-cycle is immediate. To
show that
(\Pi(\mathcal{G}),\ell(\Pi(\mathcal{G})))
is incorrect, we must also
check that it is chordless, that is, we must rule out the possibility that any
edge in
R_{1}\cup R_{2}
is a chord.

•

Let
e=(a_{u,v},a_{x,y})\in R_{1}
, which means that
(u,x)\in B_{\mathcal{G}}
.
Suppose that
a_{u,v}
is part of the cycle in
(\mkern-4.0mu\llbracket\Pi(\mathcal{G})\rrbracket\mkern-4.0mu)

(otherwise,
e
is not a chord). Then
u
is part of the original cycle in

\mathcal{G}
, i.e.
u=u_{i}
for some
i\in\{0,\dots,n-1\}
. Since the cycle is

\mathcal{G}
is alternating,
x=u_{i-1}
or
x=u_{i+1}
(with indexing modulo
n
)
depending on the parity of
i
, and since it is elementary, this is the only
time
x
is visited. So there exists a unique
z\in V_{\mathcal{G}}
such that

a_{x,z}
belongs to the cycle in
(\mkern-4.0mu\llbracket\Pi(\mathcal{G})\rrbracket\mkern-4.0mu)
. We then have
either
(a_{x,z},a_{u,v})\in R_{1}
or
(a_{u,v},a_{x,z})\in R_{1}
. A case
analysis depending on whether
z=y
then shows that
e
cannot be a chord.

•

The endpoints of edges in
R_{2}
are not incident to any other
non-matching edges, so
R_{2}
cannot provide any chord.

Conversely, one can show that any æ-cycle in
(\mkern-4.0mu\llbracket\Pi(\mathcal{G})\rrbracket\mkern-4.0mu)

induces in a canonical way an alternating cycle in
\mathcal{G}
, and if one starts
from a chordless æ-cycle, then the resulting cycle is elementary.

Before moving to the last missing ingredient for the proof
of Theorem
46
, let us draw a connection with some previous material
in this paper.

{exa}

Consider the digraph with perfect matching on the right of

Figure

9
. Since it has no æ-cycle, its
proofification below is a provable formula in pomset logic.

{[{[{a\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}b}]\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise
