0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}d^{\bot}}]}

This is very close to the counterexample presented in

Section

3.2
. In fact, we have already seen an abridged drawing
for the corresponding proof net in that section, in

Figure

10(c)
. In general, our complexity results imply
that unless
\mathbf{NP}=\mathbf{coNP}
, there must exist some proofification that is
provable in pomset logic but not in
{\mathsf{BV}}
, and this provides an explicit
example. Furthermore, if we forget the edge directions in this graph (right of

Figure

9
) and then take its proofification, we get the

“linear medial”
of Remark
37
/
Figure

10(a)
.

4.4.
Finding Alternating Elementary Cycles is
\mathbf{NP}
-Hard

The technical heart of our proof of
\mathbf{coNP}
-hardness for pomset proof net
correctness is:

Theorem 50
.

Deciding the existence of æ-cycles in RB-digraphs is
\mathbf{NP}
-hard.

We shall prove this by a many-one polynomial time reduction from the
cnf-sat

problem described in
Section

4.1
. A more concise proof would
have been possible by relying on a result on
edge-colored
digraphs

[
GLMM13
, Theorem 5]
(this is actually how we
discovered the theorem, and this proof can be found in
[
Ngu19
]
).
Nevertheless, our direct reduction, which is heavily inspired by
16
16

16

One
step in the proof of
[
GLMM13
, Theorem 5]
consists of
adding edge directions to a reduction from digraphs to undirected
2-edge-colored graphs (called Häggkvist’s transformation); morally, our
reduction replaces this with a well-known correspondence between digraphs and
undirected RB-graphs (something of this kind is implicit, for instance, in the
use of the classical Ford-Fulkerson maximum flow algorithm to compute a
maximum matching).

[
GLMM13
]
, has two advantages: it
shortens the chain of dependencies, and lends itself to being adapted into the

\mathbf{\Sigma_{2}^{p}}
-hardness proof
17
17

17

This is why we use a reduction from

cnf-sat
, whereas the proof of
[
GLMM13
, Theorem 5]

consists of a reduction between two graph-theoretic problems—no Boolean
formulas are mentioned anywhere in
[
GLMM13
]
.
of our
next subsection.

In a nutshell

Let us first give a rough idea of the proof, illustrated by figures on the

cnf-sat
instance
(x\lor y\lor z)\land(\lnot x\lor y)\land(\lnot y\lor\lnot z)
.

We first build a digraph
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{cl}}
(
Figure

12(a)
) with two distinguished
vertices
s
and
t
such that paths from
s
to
t
are in bijection with

choices of one literal per clause
. To make this work, the graph contains
one vertex for each literal occurrence.

Next, we build
on the same set of vertices
a digraph
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{var}}

(
Figure

12(b)
) such that paths from
t
to
s
correspond bijectively to
variable assignments in the following way: the path traverses all the literal
occurrences set to
\mathtt{false}
. The point is that if we manage to go from

s
to
t
with a path in
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{cl}}
, and then go back from
t
to
s

with a path in
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{var}}

avoiding all vertices that were already
visited
, then the first path will only select literals set to
\mathtt{true}

by the second path. This means that cycles visiting both
s
and
t
yield

satisfying assignments
and vice versa.

Finally, the tricky part is to reduce finding such a cycle with two prescribed
vertices to finding an æ-cycle in an RB-digraph.
This is done by a generic construction (
Figure

13
) that morally

“superimposes”
in some way these two graphs
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{cl}}
and

\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{var}}
. It requires a few additional conditions, among which the
acyclicity of both
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{cl}}
and
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{var}}
.

x
\lnot x
y
\lnot y
z
\lnot z
x\lor y\lor z
\lnot x\lor y
\lnot y\lor\lnot z
s
t

(a)

The
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{cl}}
construction (Lemma
51
).

x
\lnot x
y
\lnot y
z
\lnot z
x\lor y\lor z
\lnot x\lor y
\lnot y\lor\lnot z
s
t

(b)

The
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{var}}
construction (Lemma
52
).

Figure 12
.
The reduction for the proof of Theorem
50
on the CNF
(x\lor y\lor z)\land(\lnot x\lor y)\land(\lnot y\lor\lnot z)
. The
thick colored paths correspond: in
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{cl}}
(left), to selecting the
literals
z,\lnot x,\lnot y
in the successive clauses; in

\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{var}}
(right), to the assignment
x=\mathtt{false}
,
y=\mathtt{false}
,
z=\mathtt{true}
which makes the selected literals true. Observe that the two paths do
not share any intermediate vertex. It is also worth paying attention to the
horizontal edge for
y
on the right, that showcases how to handle multiple
occurrences of a literal set to
\mathtt{false}
.

Proof details

For the remainder of this subsection, we fix an instance of
cnf-sat
, presented
formally as in
Section

4.1
: it is a finite ordered set of

clauses

\{C_{1},\ldots,C_{n}\}
; each clause is a finite ordered set of

literals

C_{i}=\{l_{i,1},\ldots,l_{i,m(i)}\}
; finally, each literal is
either
x
or
\lnot x

for some
variable

x\in X=\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{p}\}
. Given this instance,
we consider a set of vertices
V_{\mathrm{occ}}=\{v_{i,j}\mid(i,j)\in I\}
with
one vertex for each literal occurrence (thus,
I=\{(i,j)\in\mathbb{N}^{2}\mid 1\leq i\leq n,\,1\leq j\leq m(i)\}
), plus two auxiliary
vertices
s
and
t
(outside the set
V_{\mathrm{occ}}
).

Recall that a digraph is said to be
acyclic
when it contains no cycles.
An observation to keep in mind for the three following lemmas is that a path in
an acyclic digraph is always elementary.

Lemma 51

(see
Figure

12(a)
)
.

From the given
cnf-sat
instance, one can build in polynomial time a directed
graph
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{cl}}=(V_{\mathrm{occ}}\cup\{s,t\},E_{\mathrm{cl}})
such that:

•

\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{cl}}
is acyclic,
s
has no incoming
edges and
t
has no outgoing edges;

•

each path from
s
to
t
in
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{cl}}
visits exactly the intermediate
vertices
\{v_{i,j[i]}\mid 1\leq i\leq n\}
for some
j[1],\ldots,j[n]

with
1\leq j[i]\leq m(i)
;

•

conversely, every such choice of one literal per clause induces a unique
path from
s
to
t
, visiting exactly the corresponding vertices.

Proof 4.6
.

As illustrated in
Figure

12(a)
, we take:

\displaystyle E_{\mathrm{cl}}~~=~~

\displaystyle\big\{(s,v_{1,j})\mid j\in\{1,\ldots,m(1)\}\big\}

\displaystyle~~\cup~~

\displaystyle\big\{(v_{i,j},v_{i+1,j^{\prime}})\mid i\in\{1,\ldots,n-1\},\,j\in\{1,\ldots,m(i)\},\,j^{\prime}\in\{1,\ldots,m(i+1)\}\big\}

\displaystyle~~\cup~~

\displaystyle\big\{(v_{n,j},t)\mid j\in\{1,\ldots,m(n)\}\big\}

It is straightforward to check that the required properties hold. For
instance, the absence of cycles in
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{cl}}
is a consequence of the
following fact: for all
(v_{i,j},v_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}})\in E_{\mathrm{cl}}
we have
i<i^{\prime}
.

Lemma 52

(see
Figure

12(b)
)
.

From the given
cnf-sat
instance, assuming without loss of generality that each
variable has at least one positive and one negative occurrence (see
Remark
38
), one can build in polynomial time a
digraph
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{var}}
such that:

•

\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{var}}=(V_{\mathrm{occ}}\cup\{s,t\},E_{\mathrm{var}})
is acyclic,
t
has no incoming edges and
s
has no outgoing
edges (note that the roles of
t
and
s
are reversed compared to
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{cl}}
);

•

for each path from
t
to
s
, the set of intermediate vertices in

\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{var}}
that it visits is of the form
\{v_{i,j}\mid(i,j)\in I,\;l_{i,j}\in\{\lnot x\mid x\in Y\}\cup(X\setminus Y)\}
for a
unique set of variables
Y\subseteq X
;

•

conversely, every such subset of variables corresponds to a (unique) path
from
t
to
s
.

One should see such a
Y\subseteq X
above as an

assignment

\chi_{Y}:X\to\{\mathtt{true},\mathtt{false}\}
, with
Y=\chi_{Y}^{-1}(\{\mathtt{true}\})
. So the
vertices traversed correspond to the literals set to
\mathtt{false}
.

Proof 4.7
.

Let us first describe what the paths starting from
t
will look like once we
have defined the digraph. First, we have to choose
l_{1}\in\{x_{1},\lnot x_{1}\}
and go to its first occurrence (first for the order induced by the
clauses). Then as long as we are on an occurrence of
l_{1}
which is not the
last one, there is a single outgoing edge, and it leads to the next occurrence.
Finally, once the last occurrence of
l_{1}
is reached, we may go to the first
occurrence of
l_{2}
for some choice
l_{2}\in\{x_{2},\lnot x_{2}\}
. And so on,
until the last occurrence of either
x_{p}
or
\lnot x_{p}
which finally allows
us to arrive at
s
.

To enforce this, we define
E_{\mathrm{var}}
(as shown in
Figure

12(b)
)
to consist of all the edges:

•

(v_{i,j},v_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}})
such that
l_{i,j}=l_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}=l
and the
occurrence of
l
in
C_{i^{\prime}}
is the successor of its occurrence in
C_{i}
,
i.e.
i<i^{\prime}
and
i<i^{\prime\prime}<i^{\prime}\implies l\notin C_{i^{\prime\prime}}
;

•

(v_{i,j},v_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}})
such that for some
(l_{i,j},l_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}})\in\bigcup_{1\leq k\leq p-1}(\{x_{k},\lnot x_{k}\}\times\{x_{k+1},\lnot x_{k+1}\})
,
C_{i}
is the last clause containing a literal equal to

l_{i,j}
while
C_{i^{\prime}}
is the first clause containing
l_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}
;

•

(t,v_{i,j})
and
(t,v_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}})
, where
l_{i,j}=x_{1}
,

l_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}=\lnot x_{1}
and
C_{i},C_{i^{\prime}}
are the first clauses in which
those literals appear respectively;

•

(v_{i,j},s)
and
(v_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}},s)
for the last occurrences

l_{i,j},l_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}
of
x_{p},\lnot x_{p}
.

\underline{s}
u
v
w
\underline{t}
\underline{s}
u
v
w
\underline{t}
s_{1}
s_{2}
+
-
+
-
+
-
t_{1}
t_{2}
u
v
w

Figure 13
.
Example for the proof of Lemma
53
. On the left, two
digraphs are drawn on the same vertices. On the right, the RB-digraph
obtained by applying the construction of Lemma
53
with the
distinguished vertices
s
and
t
. (We do not reuse the graphs in

Figure

12
because the drawing of their
“superposition”

would be unreadable.) The pair of paths
(s\to u\to v\to t,\;t\to w\to s)
on the left corresponds to an æ-cycle on the right, whereas the

“short-circuit”

(s\to u\to w,\;w\to s)
– which we want to
exclude – corresponds to a cycle that is not alternating (the consecutive
edges
u^{\ominus}\to w^{\oplus}\to s_{2}
are both outside the matching).

Lemma 53

(see
Figure

13
)
.

Let
\mathcal{G}_{1}=(V,E_{1})
and
\mathcal{G}_{2}=(V,E_{2})
be two directed graphs with the same
vertex set
V
. Let
s,t\in V
.
Assume that
\mathcal{G}_{1}
and
\mathcal{G}_{2}
are acyclic and that
s
(resp.
t
)
has no incoming edge in
\mathcal{G}_{1}
(resp.
\mathcal{G}_{2}
) and no outgoing
edge in
\mathcal{G}_{2}
(resp.
\mathcal{G}_{1}
). Then one can build in polynomial
time an RB-digraph
\mathcal{H}
whose æ-cycles are in bijection with the
pairs
(P_{1},P_{2})
where:

•

P_{1}
is a path from
s
to
t
in
\mathcal{G}_{1}
;

•

P_{2}
is a path from
t
to
s
in
\mathcal{G}_{2}
;

•

P_{1}\setminus\{s,t\}
and
P_{2}\setminus\{s,t\}
are vertex-disjoint.

Proof 4.8
.

Our construction of
\mathcal{H}=(V_{\mathcal{H}},R_{\mathcal{H}},B_{\mathcal{H}})

associates to each original vertex a
B
-edge:

\displaystyle V_{\mathcal{H}}

\displaystyle=\left\{s_{1},s_{2},t_{1},t_{2}\right\}\cup\left\{v^{p}\mid v\in V,\;p\in\{\oplus,\ominus\}\right\}\quad\text{where}\ V^{\prime}=V\setminus\{s,t\}

\displaystyle B_{\mathcal{H}}

\displaystyle=\left\{(s_{1},s_{2}),(s_{2},s_{1}),(t_{1},t_{2}),(t_{2},t_{1})\right\}\cup\left\{(v^{p},v^{q})\;\middle|\;v\in V,\;(p,q)\in\{(\oplus,\ominus),(\ominus,\oplus)\}\right\}

The non-matching edges are obtained from the original edges: writing
E_{i}

for the set of edges of
\mathcal{G}_{i}
(for
i\in\{1,2\}
), we take
R_{\mathcal{H}}=R^{\prime}_{1}\cup R^{\prime}_{2}
where

\displaystyle R^{\prime}_{1}=

\displaystyle\left\{(s_{1},v^{\oplus})\;\middle|\;v\in V^{\prime}\ \text{such that}\ (s,v)\in E_{1}\right\}\cup\left\{(u^{\ominus},t_{1})\;\middle|\;u\in V^{\prime}\ \text{such that}\ (u,t)\in E_{1}\right\}

\displaystyle\cup

\displaystyle\left\{(u^{\ominus},v^{\oplus})\;\middle|\;u,v\in V^{\prime}\ \text{such that}\ (u,v)\in E_{1}\right\}

\displaystyle R^{\prime}_{2}=

\displaystyle\left\{(t_{2},v^{\ominus})\;\middle|\;v\in V^{\prime}\ \text{such that}\ (t,v)\in E_{2}\right\}\cup\left\{(u^{\oplus},s_{2})\;\middle|\;u\in V^{\prime}\ \text{such that}\ (u,t)\in E_{2}\right\}

\displaystyle\cup

\displaystyle\left\{(u^{\oplus},v^{\ominus})\;\middle|\;u,v\in V^{\prime}\ \text{such that}\ (u,v)\in E_{2}\right\}

so that
B
,
R^{\prime}_{1}
and
R^{\prime}_{2}
are pairwise disjoint.

Given a pair of paths
(P_{1},P_{2})
with
P_{1}=s\to u_{1}\to\dots\to u_{r}\to t
and
P_{2}=t\to v_{1}\to\dots\to v_{q}\to s
, as specified
in the lemma statement, we can build an æ-cycle in
\mathcal{H}
,
where ‘
\Rightarrow
’ denotes a matching edge:

s_{1}\to u_{1}^{\oplus}\Rightarrow u_{1}^{\ominus}\to u_{2}^{\oplus}\Rightarrow\dots\Rightarrow u_{r}^{\ominus}\to t_{1}\Rightarrow t_{2}\to v_{1}^{\ominus}\Rightarrow v_{1}^{\oplus}\to v_{2}^{\ominus}\Rightarrow\dots\Rightarrow v_{q}^{\oplus}\to s_{2}\Rightarrow s_{1}

It is alternating by construction, and it is elementary because
P_{1}
and
P_{2}

themselves are necessarily elementary (they are paths in acyclic digraphs).

Conversely, we want to extract such a pair of paths
(P_{1},P_{2})
from any æ-cycle in

\mathcal{H}
. First, we claim that the RB-digraph
(V_{\mathcal{H}},R^{\prime}_{1},B)

does not contain any æ-cycle. This is because any æ-path of length
\geq 2
in
it is strictly increasing for the following order: the lexicographic product of
the transitive closure of
E_{1}
— which, by acyclicity assumption on

\mathcal{G}_{1}
, is a partial order — with the order on
\{\oplus,\ominus\}

defined by
\oplus\leq\ominus
. Likewise,
(V_{\mathcal{H}},R^{\prime}_{2},B)
does
not admit any æ-cycle either. Therefore, an æ-cycle in
\mathcal{H}
must contain two
edges
e_{1}\in R^{\prime}_{1}
and
e_{2}\in R^{\prime}_{2}
. Given such an æ-cycle, let

\pi_{1}
be the directed subpath starting with
e_{1}
and ending with
e_{2}
, and
\pi_{2}
be the complementary directed subpath starting with
e_{2}
and ending with
e_{1}
.
Then:

•

\pi_{1}
contains a subpath
v_{1}\xrightarrow{\;R^{\prime}_{1}\;}v_{2}\xRightarrow{\;B\;}v_{3}\xrightarrow{\;R^{\prime}_{2}\;}v_{4}
. Since

v_{2}
is the target of an edge in
E_{1}
, either
v_{2}=t_{2}
or
v_{2}=v^{\oplus}
for some
v\in V
. In the latter case, we have
v_{3}=v^{\ominus}
, which is impossible for the source of an edge in
E_{2}
.
Therefore
(v_{2},v_{3})=(t_{1},t_{2})
.

•

\pi_{2}
contains a subpath
u_{1}\xrightarrow{\;R^{\prime}_{2}\;}u_{2}\xRightarrow{\;B\;}u_{3}\xrightarrow{\;R^{\prime}_{1}\;}u_{4}
. Similarly to the
previous case, we conclude that
(u_{2},u_{3})=(s_{2},s_{1})
.

To recapitulate: the cycle must switch at some point from edges in
R^{\prime}_{1}

to edges in
R^{\prime}_{2}
, and it must also switch back at some point; it can
only do the former by crossing
(t_{1},t_{2})\in B
and the latter by
crossing
(s_{2},s_{1})\in B
. Therefore, this æ-cycle decomposes into
an æ-path
P_{1}
from
s_{1}
to
t_{1}
in
(V_{\mathcal{H}},R^{\prime}_{1},B)
and an
æ-path
P_{2}
from
t_{2}
to
s_{2}
in
(V_{\mathcal{H}},R^{\prime}_{2},B)
, glued
together by
(t_{1},t_{2})\in B
and
(s_{2},s_{1})\in B
. These paths
are vertex-disjoint because the æ-cycle that they form is, by definition of
æ-cycle, elementary; they can be lifted to yield the desired pair of paths in

\mathcal{G}_{1}
and
\mathcal{G}_{2}
.

We now combine these ingredients to reduce
cnf-sat
to
the directed æ-cycle problem.

Proof 4.9

(Proof of Theorem
50
)
.

We apply the construction of the previous lemma to
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{cl}}
and

\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{var}}
(with
V=V_{\mathrm{occ}}
). An æ-cycle in the
resulting RB-digraph corresponds to a path
P_{\mathrm{cl}}
from

s
to
t
in
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{cl}}
plus a path
P_{\mathrm{var}}
from
t
to
s
in

\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{var}}
, that are vertex-disjoint except at
s
and
t
. We have to
show that the existence of the latter is equivalent to that of an assignment
that
satisfies all the clauses
.

Suppose that we are given such an assignment. First, there exists a unique
path
P_{\mathrm{var}}
in
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{var}}
that visits all literal occurrences set
to
\mathtt{false}
(Lemma
52
). Since the assignment is satisfying, we
may choose in each clause a literal set to
\mathtt{true}
.
This corresponds by Lemma
51
to a path
P_{\mathrm{cl}}
in
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{cl}}
.
If some vertex of
V_{\mathrm{occ}}
were to appear in both
P_{\mathrm{var}}
and
P_{\mathrm{cl}}
,
it would mean that the corresponding literal is set both to
\mathtt{false}

and to
\mathtt{true}
simultaneously.

The converse direction proceeds by a similar reasoning.

4.5.
Pomset Logic Provability is
\mathbf{\Sigma_{2}^{p}}
-Complete

We are now in a position to treat our main complexity result:

Theorem 54
.

The provability problem of pomset logic is
\mathbf{\Sigma_{2}^{p}}
-complete.

As
in the case of correctness, we start with the membership part whose proof is
easier than the hardness part.

Proposition 55
.

Pomset logic provability is in
\mathbf{\Sigma_{2}^{p}}
.

Proof 4.10
.

The size of any pre-proof (i.e. axiom linking) is bounded by a polynomial in
the size of its conclusion, and the correctness criterion is in
\mathbf{coNP}
.
Therefore, there is a
\mathbf{\Sigma_{2}^{p}}=\mathbf{NP}^{\mathbf{NP}}
algorithm that consists in first
guessing non-deterministically a pre-proof whose conclusion is the input
formula, then calling a
\mathbf{NP}
oracle for incorrectness on this guess, and
finally accepting whenever the oracle returns
\mathtt{false}
.

To show the
\mathbf{\Sigma_{2}^{p}}
-hardness of provability, we proceed analogously to the

\mathbf{coNP}
-hardness proof for correctness, using a sequence of reductions

\text{Boolean formula problem}\longrightarrow\text{graph-theoretic
problem}\longrightarrow\text{pomset logic problem}

Here, this pattern is instantiated with the
\mathbf{\Pi_{2}^{p}}
-complete
\forall\exists
-cnf-sat

problem (cf.
Section

4.1
) to the left and
unprovability

in pomset logic to the right. For the auxiliary step in the middle, we use a
problem formulated using
“switchings”
of
“paired graphs”
, as in
the standard Danos–Regnier correctness criterion for
\mathord{\mathsf{MLL\!+\!mix}}

[
DR89
,
FR94
]
.

{defi}

A
paired digraph
is a directed graph
\mathcal{G}
equipped with a set

\mathfrak{P}
of unordered pairs of edges, such that the pairs are disjoint (if

p,p^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{P}
, then
p\cap p^{\prime}=\varnothing
) and paired edges have
the same source (if
\{(u_{1},v_{1}),(u_{2},v_{2})\}\in\mathfrak{P}
, then
u_{1}=u_{2}
). An edge is
unpaired
if it is not an element of any pair in

\mathfrak{P}
. A
switching
18
18

18

The literature sometimes uses

“switching”
for a choice function on the set of edge pairs, rather
than the digraph defined by this choice.
of
(\mathcal{G},\mathfrak{P})
is a
spanning subgraph of
\mathcal{G}
that contains all unpaired edges and exactly
one edge in each pair.

A
paired RB-digraph
is a tuple
(V_{\mathcal{G}},R_{\mathcal{G}},B_{\mathcal{G}},\mathfrak{P})
such that

(V_{\mathcal{G}},R_{\mathcal{G}},B_{\mathcal{G}})
is a RB-digraph and
(V_{\mathcal{G}},R_{\mathcal{G}},\mathfrak{P})
is a paired
digraph. Consistently with the above, we define a
switching
of

(V_{\mathcal{G}},R_{\mathcal{G}},B_{\mathcal{G}},\mathfrak{P})
to be an RB-digraph of the form

(V_{\mathcal{G}},R^{\prime},B_{\mathcal{G}},\mathfrak{P})
where
R^{\prime}\subseteq R_{\mathcal{G}}
contains all unpaired edges
from
R_{\mathcal{G}}
and exactly one edge in each pair.

The reader familiar with proof nets might find it strange that we are mixing
perfect matchings and paired graphs together: the former already play the role
of expressing the correctness criterion, so what are the latter good for? The
answer is that switchings will morally correspond to choices of axiom linkings:
the possible proof nets whose conclusion is a given arbitrary (not necessarily
balanced) formula only differ by which pairs of dual atoms are joined by axiom
links. Without further ado, let us present our reductions.

x
\lnot x
y
\lnot y
z
\lnot z
x\lor y\lor z
\lnot x\lor y
\lnot y\lor\lnot z
s
t

Figure 14
.
The paired
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{var}}
built from
\forall x\;\forall y\;\exists z\;(x\lor y\lor z)\land(\lnot x\lor y)\land(\lnot y\lor\lnot z)
in the proof of
Lemma
56
(compare with
Figure

12(b)
). The dashed edges
represent the choice of a switching in which these edges are erased.
The specific choice made here gives us three bits of information that can be
interpreted as
x=\mathtt{false}
,
(x=\mathtt{true})\Rightarrow(y=\mathtt{true})
and finally
(x=\mathtt{false})\Rightarrow(y=\mathtt{false})
– recall that visiting a literal corresponds
to setting it to
\mathtt{false}
– which entails
x=y=\mathtt{false}
. The
thick colored path materializes this assignment of the
\forall
-variables,
and it is a prefix of all paths from
t
to
s
in this switching.

Lemma 56
.

It is
\mathbf{\Pi_{2}^{p}}
-hard to decide whether
every
switching of a paired
RB-digraph contains
at least one
æ-cycle.

Proof 4.11
.

We proceed by polynomial time reduction from
\forall\exists
-cnf-sat
(cf.
Section

4.1
). Consider an instance of this problem whose
universal variables are
x_{1},\ldots,x_{m}
(we will not need to directly
manipulate the CNF or the existential variables). We reuse the constructions

\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{cl}}
and
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{var}}
of Lemmas
51

and
52
, applying them to the CNF part of the input. Strictly
speaking, the digraph
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{var}}
depends on the order of variables,
and here we shall consider for convenience that the universal variables come
before the existential ones, with the
i
-th variable being
x_{i}
for
i\leq m
.

The key idea is to distinguish a set
\mathfrak{P}
of disjoint edge pairs in

\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{var}}
(see
Figure

14
for an example). Let
t
and
s

be its distinguished source and sink vertices. For
1\leq i\leq m
, we
write:

•

u_{i}
(resp.
v_{i}
) for the vertex that corresponds to the first
(resp. last) occurrence of
x_{i}
;

•

u^{\lnot}_{i}
(resp.
v^{\lnot}_{i}
) for the vertex that corresponds to the
first (resp. last) occurrence of
\lnot x_{i}
.

(Here
“first”
and
“last”
mean the same thing as in the
construction of
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{var}}
(Lemma
52
): they are defined with
respect to an arbitrary order on the set of clauses. To build

\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{var}}
, each variable must occur at least once positively and once
negatively; this can be assumed without loss of generality for instances of

\forall\exists
-cnf-sat
. It is possible that
u_{i}=v_{i}
, but for
i\neq j
, we have

u_{i}\neq v_{j}
.) We take

\mathfrak{P}=\left\{\left\{(w,u_{i}),\,(w,u^{\lnot}_{i})\right\}\;\middle|\;i\in\{1,\ldots,m\},\;w\in\left[\begin{array}[]{l l}\{s\}&\text{when}\ i=1\\
\{v_{i-1},v^{\lnot}_{i-1}\}&\text{otherwise}\end{array}\right.\right\}

The reader may check that all edges that appear in
\mathfrak{P}
are indeed
edges in
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{var}}
. Furthermore, it follows from the definition that
the pairs are disjoint and any two paired edges have the same source.
Therefore,
(\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{var}},\mathfrak{P})
is a paired digraph in the sense of
Definition
4.5
.

The point is that, as illustrated in
Figure

14
, for any switching

\mathcal{S}
of
(\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{var}},\mathfrak{P})
, exactly one of
v_{m}

and
v^{\lnot}_{m}
is reachable from
s
, and the path is unique – let us call
it
\pi_{\mathcal{S}}
. Each switching thus induces an assignment

\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{m}\}\to\{\mathtt{true},\mathtt{false}\}
, which assigns
x_{i}
to
\mathtt{false}

when
\pi_{\mathcal{S}}
visits the vertices associated to
x_{i}
(among which
are
u_{i}
and
v_{i}
), and to
\mathtt{true}
otherwise (in which case the path
necessarily visits
u^{\lnot}_{i}
and
v^{\lnot}_{i}
); and the paths from
s
to

t
in the switching correspond to extending this assignment with values for
the other (existential) variables. Moreover, this map from switchings to
assignments of the universal variables is surjective (more precisely, each of
the
2^{m}
assignments is induced by exactly
2^{m-1}
switchings among the
total of
2^{2m-1}
possible switchings).

Let us consider next the correspondence given by Lemma
52
between the
paths from
t
to
s
and the assignments of all variables (both universal and
existential). One can see that given such a path
\rho
and a switching

\mathcal{S}
, the following are equivalent:

•

the edges of
\rho
exist in
\mathcal{S}
;

•

\pi_{\mathcal{S}}
is a prefix of
\rho
;

•

the assignment that corresponds to
\mathcal{S}
(by
the above discussion) is the restriction to the universal variables of the
one that corresponds to
\rho
(by Lemma
52
).

From these observations, one can deduce, by a similar reasoning to the proof
of Theorem
50
, that the given
\forall\exists
-cnf-sat
instance is
positive if and only if, for every switching
\mathcal{S}
of

(\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{var}},\mathfrak{P})
, there exists a path from
s
to
t
in

\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{cl}}
and a path from
t
to
s
in
\mathcal{S}
that do not share
any intermediate vertex. To conclude, it suffices to reduce this to the
desired problem on paired RB-digraphs by a suitable adaptation of
Lemma
53
, whose precise formulation is left to the reader.

Theorem 57
.

Unprovability in pomset logic is
\mathbf{\Pi_{2}^{p}}
-hard.

Proof 4.12
.

We reduce the problem shown to be
\mathbf{\Pi_{2}^{p}}
-hard by Lemma
56

to pomset logic unprovability by extending proofification to handle edge
pairs. An example is given in
Figure

15
.

Let
(V_{\mathcal{G}},R_{\mathcal{G}},B_{\mathcal{G}},\mathfrak{P})
be a paired RB-digraph (we write
\mathcal{G}=(V_{\mathcal{G}},R_{\mathcal{G}},B_{\mathcal{G}})
). We assume without loss of generality that for every edge pair

\{(u,v),(u,w)\}\in\mathfrak{P}
, the opposite edges
(v,u)
and
(w,u)
are
not present in the graph (otherwise, break
(v,u)
up into a path of length
three involving two new vertices, add the middle edge of this path to the
matching, and similarly for
(w,u)
). Let us define the following flat
sequent, which is
not
balanced in general (the description deliberately
mirrors that of
\Pi(\mathcal{G})
in Definition
4.3
):

\Gamma={[{C_{u_{1}}\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}C_{v_{1}}},\dots,{C_{u_{m}}\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}C_{v_{m}}},D_{e_{1}},\dots,D_{e_{k}},D^{\prime}_{p_{1}},\dots,D^{\prime}_{p_{l}}]}

where (using again the notation of Definition
2.2
):

•

\{e_{1},\dots,e_{k}\}
is the set of
unpaired
edges,

\{(u_{1},v_{1}),(v_{1},u_{1}),\dots,(u_{m},v_{m}),(v_{m},u_{m})\}=B_{\mathcal{G}}
, and

\{p_{1},\dots,p_{l}\}=\mathfrak{P}
, all these enumerations being
non-repeating;

•

the atoms involved in
\Gamma
are created as follows:

–

for each
\{u,v\}\subseteq V_{\mathcal{G}}
such that
(u,v)\in R^{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.36165pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}}_{\mathcal{\mathcal{G}}}

– according to the assumption w.l.o.g. we made above, both
(u,v)
and

(v,u)
are then unpaired – we give the names
a_{u,v}
and
a_{v,u}
to
a fresh pair of
dual
atoms;

–

for each edge
(u,v)\in R^{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.36165pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}}_{\mathcal{\mathcal{G}}}
which is unpaired, we generate
two fresh atoms
a_{u,v}
and
a_{v,u}
that are
not
dual;

–

for each pair
p=\{(u,v),(u,w)\}\in\mathfrak{P}
, we create two
fresh atoms
a_{u,p},b_{p}
and we define
a_{v,u}=a_{w,u}=b_{p}

(observe however that we do
not
define
a_{u,v}
nor
a_{u,w}
);

•

for
u\in V_{\mathcal{G}}
,
C_{u}=\displaystyle{}_{v}a_{u,v}\invamp{}_{p}a_{u,p}
where
v
(resp.
p
) ranges over the vertices
(resp. pairs) such that
a_{u,v}
(resp.
a_{u,v}
) is defined according
to the above;

•

for
(u,v)\in R^{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.36165pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}}_{\mathcal{\mathcal{G}}}
, if
(u,v)
is unpaired, then
D_{(u,v)}={a_{u,v}^{\bot}\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}a_{v,u}^{\bot}}
;

•

finally, for
p=\{(u,v),(u,w)\}\in\mathfrak{P}
, we set
D^{\prime}_{p}={\langle{a_{u,p}^{\bot}\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}b_{p}^{\bot}}\rangle\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}b_{p}^{\bot}}
.

w
x
y
z
a_{w,x}
a_{x,w}
a_{x,y}
b_{p}^{\bot}
b_{p}^{\bot}
a_{y,p}^{\bot}
a_{y,p}
a_{z,y}

Figure 15
.
Example for the proof of Theorem
57
.

Left: a
paired RB-digraph (compare
Figure

11
); the two non-matching
edges coming out of
y
are paired, and the dashed edge represents a choice
of switching in which it is deleted.

Right: the cographic
RB-prenet corresponding to this choice, with
p=\{(y,x),(y,z)\}
(the
dashed blue/bold edges display the matching that would correspond to the
other possible choice). Note that we have
a_{x,y}=a_{z,y}=b_{p}
.

This sequent
\Gamma
that we just defined contains, for each pair
p=\{(u,v),(u,w)\}\in\mathfrak{P}
, exactly two occurrences of
b_{p}
, designated
as
a_{v,u}
and
a_{w,u}
, and exactly two occurrences of
b_{p}^{\bot}
in

D^{\prime}_{p}
, that we will also name:
D^{\prime}_{p}={\langle{a_{u,p}^{\bot}\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}c_{p\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.36165pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}}}\rangle\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}c_{p\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.36165pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}}}
. So each pre-proof of
\Gamma

induces a bijection between
\{a_{v,u},a_{w,u}\}
and

\{c_{p\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.36165pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}},c_{p\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.36165pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}}\}
. Conversely, such a choice of bijection for each
edge pair uniquely determines a corresponding axiom linking.

Let
\ell
be a pre-proof of
\Gamma
. Let
s,t:\mathfrak{P}\to V_{\mathcal{G}}
be
defined by
\ell(c_{p\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.36165pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}})=a_{t(p),s(p)}
for all
p\in\mathfrak{P}
.
We define the RB-digraph
\mathcal{S}_{\ell}
to be the switching where the
edge
(s(p),t(p))
has been deleted in each pair
p
. We can reformulate what
we observed in the previous paragraph as the fact that
\ell\mapsto\mathcal{S}_{\ell}
is a bijection from axiom linkings for
\Gamma
to
switchings of our paired RB-digraph.

In the cographic RB-prenet
\rho(\Gamma,\ell)
, the vertex for

c_{p\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.36165pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}}
has no incident non-matching edge, so it cannot be involved in
any æ-cycle. Since it is matched with
a_{t(p),s(p)}
, the latter also cannot
occur in any æ-cycle. So the witnesses of incorrectness in

\rho(\Gamma,\ell)
, i.e. its chordless æ-cycles, must be entirely
contained in the induced sub-RB-digraph that excludes
c_{p\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.36165pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}}
and

a_{t(p),s(p)}
for all
p\in\mathfrak{P}
. Thanks to the correspondence
between pseudo-subformulas and induced subgraphs
(Proposition
29
), one can write (an isomorphic copy of) this
sub-RB-digraph as
\rho(\Gamma^{\prime},\ell^{\prime})
where
\Gamma^{\prime}
is obtained from

\Gamma
by substituting the aforementioned atom occurrences by
\mathord{\mathbb{I}}
, and

\ell^{\prime}
is a restriction of
\ell
; so
(\Gamma,\ell)
is
incorrect iff
(\Gamma^{\prime},\ell^{\prime})
is.

The key property, that one can check from the definitions, is that
\Gamma^{\prime}

is balanced and equal up to atom renaming to the proofification

\Pi(\mathcal{S}_{\ell})
(note for instance that if
(u,v)\in p\in\mathfrak{P}
and
(u,v)\in R_{\mathcal{\mathcal{S}_{\ell}}}
then the formula

D^{\prime}_{p}
in
\Gamma
becomes
D_{(u,v)}
in
\Pi(\mathcal{S}_{\ell})
after
substituting
c_{p\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.36165pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}}
by
\mathord{\mathbb{I}}
). Thus, by Theorem
49
,

(\Gamma,\ell)
is incorrect if and only if
\mathcal{S}_{\ell}
contains
an æ-cycle. By definition of provability, and using the surjectivity of

\ell\mapsto\mathcal{S}_{\ell}
, it follows that
\Gamma
is unprovable if
and only if every switching of the initial RB-graph admits an æ-cycle.
According to Lemma
56
, it is
\mathbf{NP}
-hard to test the latter
condition given the input
\Gamma
. Since
\Gamma
can be computed from our
original paired RB-digraph in polynomial time, this concludes the proof.

Proposition
55
and Theorem
57
together are
what it means, by definition, for provability in pomset logic to be

\mathbf{\Sigma_{2}^{p}}
-complete. Thus, we have established Theorem
54
.

5.
Back to the Sequent Calculus

In this section we come back to the problem of finding a sequent calculus for
{\mathsf{BV}}
and pomset logic, as this problem was the starting point for much of the research done on these two logics. The current state of the art on this topic is:

(1)

Retoré
[
Ret93
]
presented a sequent calculus for pomset logic,
for which he could show soundness, but not
completeness and not cut elimination.
19
19

19

In fact, in
[
Ret97a
]
and
[
Ret21
]
two variations of that sequent calculus are proposed, which both have the same problem.

(2)

This difficulty motivated Gugliemi
[
Gug07
]
to develop
system
{\mathsf{BV}}
, which has a cut elimination proof in deep inference,
but not in the sequent calculus. Then Tiu
[
Tiu06
]
has
shown that
“deepness”
is necessary for
{\mathsf{BV}}
and therefore there
cannot be a sequent calculus for
{\mathsf{BV}}
in which all rules are
“shallow”
(see also Remark
45
).

(3)

However, the formulas used by Tiu
[
Tiu06
]
to defy the sequent calculus can be proved in the cut-free version of
Retoré’s
[
Ret93
]
sequent calculus, using the
entropy
rule.

(4)

In Remark
45
we also observed that our complexity results pose a much harder obstacle to a sequent calculus for pomset logic than the mere need of
“deepness”
.

(5)

But recently, Slavnov
[
Sla19
]
presented a cut-free sequent
calculus that is sound and complete for pomset logic.

This is, of course, confusing, as it seems contradictory. Does (
3
) mean that
Tiu’s
[
Tiu06
]
result is wrong? Does (
5
) mean that
Retoré
[
Ret93
]
just did not try hard enough and the problem
is solved? Does it mean that our complexity result about the
\mathbf{\Sigma_{2}^{p}}
-completeness of pomset logic is wrong or that
\mathbf{NP}=\mathbf{coNP}
?

The answer to all these questions is, of course,
“No”
. And the purpose of
this section is to clarify the confusion and work out the subtleties of
possible sequent calculi for pomset logic and
{\mathsf{BV}}
. More precisely we
present the following results:

•

We show that Retoré’s sequent calculus with cut (the variant
presented in
[
Ret21
]
) is equivalent to
{\mathsf{SBV}}
. It is
therefore a sequent calculus for
{\mathsf{BV}}
and not for pomset logic.

•

We present a formula
20
20

20

This formula has already been
presented in
[
Ret21
]
.
that is provable in
{\mathsf{BV}}
but not in the
cut-free version of Retoré’s
[
Ret93
]
sequent
calculus. Therefore, that sequent calculus does not admit cut
elimination.

•

We refine Tiu’s
[
Tiu06
]
argument about the need for

“deepness”
in
{\mathsf{BV}}
such that Retoré’s
entropy
rule is no
longer enough to prove the formulas used in the argument.

•

We use our results from the previous section to make a
complexity-theoretic argument to show that a
“standard”
sequent
calculus for pomset logic is impossible.

•

Finally, we discuss Slavnov’s
[
Sla19
]
sequent calculus
and exhibit how he circumvents this complexity-theoretic obstacle.

5.1.
Retoré’s Sequent Calculus with Cuts is Equivalent to
{\mathsf{BV}}

Recall that a pomset logic sequent can be seen as a
\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}
-free generalized formula over a set of formula occurrences (see Proposition
3
). Hence, we can define the graph of a sequent
\Gamma
as in Definition
2.2
. Let
\mathcal{G}_{\Gamma}=(V_{\Gamma},R_{\Gamma})
and
\mathcal{G}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}=(V_{\Gamma^{\prime}},R_{\Gamma^{\prime}})
be the graphs of sequents
\Gamma
and
\Gamma^{\prime}
, respectively. Then both are series-parallel orders (see Proposition
10
), and we define

\Gamma^{\prime}\preccurlyeq\Gamma\qquad\mbox{iff}\qquad V_{\Gamma^{\prime}}=V_{\Gamma}\quad\mbox{and}\quad R_{\Gamma^{\prime}}\subseteq R_{\Gamma}

(13)

i.e., both sequents contain the same formula occurrences, and the order induced by
\Gamma^{\prime}
is contained in the order induced by
\Gamma
. We are now ready to discuss Retoré’s sequent calculus, shown in Figure
16
.
21
21

21

In this calculus, which is taken from
[
Ret21
]
sequents define SP-orders. In
[
Ret93
]
, sequent can be arbitrary orders, and in
[
Ret97a
]
, the entropy rule is missing.

The
\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}
-introduction rule and the
\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}
-introduction rule simply
express the correspondence between the structural/sequent level
connectives and the logical/formula level connectives. Note that the

\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}
-introduction rule and the cut rule can only be applied in
flat contexts (but
\Gamma
and
\Delta
can be non-flat
sequents). The standard mix-rule is derivable using the dimix- and
entropy-rules.

{[a,a^{\bot}]}

{[{\langle c;b^{\bot}\rangle},{\langle c^{\bot};e^{\bot}\rangle};{\langle e;b\rangle}]}

\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.68887pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.68887pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.41331pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}

Before embarking on the equivalence of this sequent calculus with

{\mathsf{BV}}
, let us illustrate the added power of cuts. For this purpose, we
recall an example sequent considered by Retoré and the second
author:

{[a\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}(c\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}b^{\bot}),a^{\bot}\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}f,c^{\bot}\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}d^{\bot},d\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}(e^{\bot}\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}f^{\bot}),e\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}b]}

(14)

The corresponding formula of the sequent above has been shown to be
provable in
{\mathsf{BV}}
in
Figure

5
, and its cographic
RB-prenet may be found in
[
Ret21
, Figure 10]
.

Proposition 58

(Retoré & Straßburger
[
Ret21
, Proposition 5]
)
.

The sequent in (
14
) is not provable in Retoré’s sequent calculus without cuts.

The reason for this lies in the fact that in order to have a cut-free sequent proof, the
\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}
-rule has to be applied to one of the two
\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}
-formulas eventually. But the
\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}
-rule is too weak to split the context correctly.
However, with cut, the sequent is provable, as shown in
Figure

17
. We have therefore shown the following.

Corollary 59
.

Cut-elimination does
not
hold for Retoré’s sequent calculus.

Let us now establish the equivalence between
{\mathsf{SBV}}
and the sequent calculus in
Figure

16
. For this, we resort again to
\mathsf{SBVu}
(see Proposition
17
). We begin by showing that every formula that is provable in
\mathsf{SBVu}
is also provable in Retoré’s sequent calculus with cut.

Lemma 60
.

Let
{[A,B]}
and
{[C,D]}
be provable sequents. Then the sequents

{[A\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}C,B\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}D]}
and
{[A\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}C,B\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}D]}
are also provable.

.

The following derivations work:

{[A,B]}

{[C,D]}

\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.68887pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.68887pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.41331pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}

For the derivation on the right, the validity of the entropy rule depends on
an inclusion between series-parallel orders which can be mechanically
verified.

Lemma 61

(Non-atomic identity)
.

For any formula
A
, the sequent
{[A^{\bot},A]}
is provable.

Lemma 62
.

Let
A
and
B
be formulas such that
{[A^{\bot},B]}
is provable, and

S\{\cdot\}
be a context. Then
{[S\{A\}^{\bot},S\{B\}]}
is provable.

Proof 5.2

(Proof of Lemmas
61

and
62
)
.

We prove that the following holds for any formula
A
:

(1)

{[A^{\bot},A]}
is provable;

(2)

for any formulas
B
and
C
such that
[B^{\bot},C]
is provable and any

\odot\in\{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}},\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}},\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}\}
, the sequents
{[(A\odot B)^{\bot},A\odot C]}
and
{[(B\odot A)^{\bot},C\odot A]}
are provable.

Lemma
61
corresponds to item (i), while item (ii) entails
Lemma
62
by a routine induction on the context
S\{\cdot\}
.
The following observations suffice to prove (i) and (ii) simultaneously by
induction on
A
(invoking the axiom rule for the base case):

•

for any given
A
, (i) implies (ii) as a direct consequence of
Lemma
60
;

•

if
A
satisfies (i) and
A^{\prime}
satisfies (ii), then by applying (ii) to

A^{\prime}
with
B=C=A
, we see that for any
\odot\in\{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}},\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}},\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}\}
, the formula
A\odot A^{\prime}
satisfies (i).

Lemma 63
.

Let

A

\scriptstyle\mathsf{r}\;

B

be an instance of an inference rule in
\mathsf{SBVu}
. with
Then
{[A^{\bot},B]}
is provable in Retoré’s sequent calculus without cuts.

Proof 5.3
.

Let us consider for instance the switch rule. We start by considering the case
with atomic formulas
a,b,c
and a trivial context (
S\{\cdot\}=\{\cdot\}
):

{[{a\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}c}]\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}b}

\scriptstyle\mathsf{s}\;

{({a\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}b})\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}c}

We prove the corresponding sequent (for the switch rule, the derivation
involves only flat sequents and can therefore be carried out in
\mathord{\mathsf{MLL\!+\!mix}}
