{[a^{\bot},a]}

{[b^{\bot},b]}

{[a^{\bot},b^{\bot},a\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}b]}

{[c^{\bot},c]}

{[a^{\bot}\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}c^{\bot},b^{\bot},a\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}b,c]}

{[{{a^{\bot}\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}c^{\bot}}},b^{\bot},{{({a\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}b})\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}c}}]}

{[{{({a^{\bot}\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}c^{\bot}})\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}b^{\bot}}},{{({a\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}b})\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}c}}]}

Thanks to Lemma
61
, we can substitute formulas for atoms:
for any
A
,
B
and
C
, the sequent

{[{{({A^{\bot}\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}C^{\bot}})\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}B^{\bot}}},{{({A\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}B})\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}C}}]}
can be proved using
the above derivation where axioms are replaced by appropriate subproofs of

{[A^{\bot},A]}
,
{[B^{\bot},B]}
or
{[C^{\bot},C]}
. Then, using
Lemma
62
, we may conclude that

{[S\{{[{A\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}C}]\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}B}\}^{\bot},S\{{({A\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}B})\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}C}\}]}
is provable for any
context
S\{\cdot\}
.

This argument applies to all inference rules (except
\mathsf{ai^{\circ}\mkern-2.0mu}{\downarrow}
, which has no
premise, and therefore does not fit the pattern in the lemma statement). For
each of these rules, it therefore suffices to treat the case with atomic
formulas and trivial context.

For the
\mathrel{\equiv^{\prime}\mkern-2.0mu}
-rule, we can assume without loss of generality that
each instance is an application of exactly one of the equalities
in (
5
) (as each general instance of the
\mathrel{\equiv^{\prime}\mkern-2.0mu}
-rule
can be replaced by a finite sequence of these special instances).

We have thus reduced the desired conclusion to a
bounded search for cut-free proofs that we leave to the reader.

Theorem 64
.

If a formula is provable in
\mathsf{SBVu}
then it is also provable in
Retoré’s sequent calculus with cuts.

Proof 5.4
.

A proof of a formula
A
in
\mathsf{SBVu}
must have the form

\scriptstyle\mathsf{ai^{\circ}\mkern-2.0mu}{\downarrow}\;

{a\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}a^{\bot}}

\scriptstyle\mathsf{r}_{1}\;

B_{1}

\scriptstyle\mathsf{r}_{2}\;

\vdots

\scriptstyle\mathsf{r}_{n}\;

B_{n}

where
B_{n}=A
; we also define
B_{0}={a\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}a^{\bot}}
. For

i\in\{1,\dots,n\}
, the sequent
{[B_{i-1}^{\bot},B_{i}]}
is
provable in Retoré’s sequent calculus by
Lemma
63
. Furthermore,
{a\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}a^{\bot}}

also has a sequent calculus proof (an axiom rule followed by a

\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}
-intro). By composing all these sequent proofs with the cut rule,
one gets a proof of
A
.

For the converse, observe that the axiom, dimix and
\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}/\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}
-intro rules
are easy to simulate in
\mathsf{BVu}
. The treatment of
\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}
-intro is as usual (see

[
Str03b
, Section 3.3.]
or
[
Gug07
, §5]
for example), and cut is a

\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}
-intro followed by
\mathsf{i}\mathord{\uparrow}
(which is admissible in
\mathsf{SBVu}
).
Furthermore, it is an immediate consequence of Theorem
2.7
that
\mathsf{SBVu}
can also simulate the entropy rule. This is enough to prove the following theorem:

Theorem 65
.

If a sequent
\Gamma
is provable in Retoré’s sequent calculus
with cuts, then a formula
A
that corresponds (see Definition
8
) to
\Gamma
is
provable in
\mathsf{SBVu}
.

Corollary 66
.

Retoré’s sequent calculus with cuts is equivalent to
{\mathsf{BV}}
.

5.2.
A Refinement of Tiu’s Argument

In
[
Tiu06
]
, Tiu presents a sequence or formulas
S_{0},S_{1},S_{2},\ldots
with the following properties:

(1)

For each
n
, the formula
S_{n}
is provable in
{\mathsf{BV}}
.

(2)

In order to prove
S_{n}
, a subformula at depth
2n
has to be accessed first.

From this it follows, that there can be no
shallow
(i.e., all inference rules have have a fixed maximum depth) cut-free proof system equivalent to
{\mathsf{BV}}
. As most standard sequent calculi are shallow in that sense, the argument could be used to claim that there cannot be a
“standard”
cut-free sequent calculus for
{\mathsf{BV}}
. However, Tiu’s formulas also have the property

(3)

For each
n
, the formula
S_{n}
is
\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}
-free.

Since every
S_{n}
contains only the
\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}
and
\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}
connectives, it can be proved in Retoré’s calculus by first applying the
\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}
and
\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}
rules to transform the formula into a sequent with only atomic formulas. This sequent can then be derived from the axioms by only dimix instances and a single instance of the entropy rule.

This is not a contradiction, as the entropy rule is a
deep
rule (i.e., not shallow) in the sense above.
22
22

22

Technically speaking, the
\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}
-intro and the
\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}
-into rules are also deep in that sense, but they do not chance the RB-digraph associated to the sequent proof.
However, it raises the question whether we can have a refinement of Retoré’s sequent calculus that is complete for
{\mathsf{BV}}
and obeys cut elimination, as this is no longer ruled out by Tiu’s argument.

What we will show next is a sequence or formulas
R_{0},R_{1},R_{2},\ldots
, following the spirit of Tiu’s construction, having properties (1) and (2) above, but not (3). Consequently, for proving them, a
\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}
-subformula has to be accessed at arbitrary depth, without the possibility of globally splitting the context. This entails that a proper deep inference system is indeed needed, and a proof system in the sequent calculus layout is insufficient.

We start with the index set
I=\{0,1\}^{\ast}
, i.e., the set of all finite words with the symbols
0
and
1
. Then, our formulas are build from the propositional variables
\{a,b,c,y,z\}\times I
, written as
a_{i}
with
i\in I
.

For an index
i\in I
and two formulas
A
and
B
, we define now inductively the formula
\xi_{n}(i,A,B)
for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\xi_{0}(i,A,B)&=&{({[{a_{i}\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}b_{i}\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}A}]\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}y_{i}})\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}\langle{y_{i}^{\bot}\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}c_{i}}\rangle\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}\langle{a_{i}^{\bot}\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}z_{i}}\rangle\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}({z_{i}^{\bot}\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}[{b_{i}^{\bot}\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}c_{i}^{\bot}\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}B}]})}\\
\xi_{n+1}(i,A,B)&=&{({\xi_{n}({i0,a_{i},b_{i}\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}A})\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}y_{i}})\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}\langle{y_{i}^{\bot}\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}c_{i}}\rangle\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}\langle{a_{i}^{\bot}\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}z_{i}}\rangle\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}({z_{i}^{\bot}\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}\xi_{n}({i1,b_{i}^{\bot},c_{i}^{\bot}\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}B})})}\end{array}

We now define
R_{n}=\xi_{n}(0,\mathord{\mathbb{I}},\mathord{\mathbb{I}})
.
23
23

23

The reader familiar with
[
Tiu06
]
might note that the only difference between our
\xi_{n}
and Tiu’s
\alpha_{n}
is the addition of the variables
y_{i}
and
z_{i}
via the
\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}
. In fact,
\xi_{n}
can be derived from
\alpha_{n}
, but not the other way around.

These formulas have the following properties:

{clm}

For each
n
, the formula
R_{n}
is provable in
{\mathsf{BV}}
.

Proof 5.5
.

For every
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
i\in I
, we have a derivation

{A\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}B}

\xi_{n}(i,A,B)

. This is constructed in the same way as in the proof of Lemma 7.4 in
[
Tiu06
]
, using a derivation that is similar to the one in
Figure

5
.

{clm}

The formulas
R_{n}
are not provable in Retoré’s sequent calculus
without cuts.

Proof 5.6
.

The formula
R_{0}
is a variation of the corresponding formula of the sequent (
14
), and the same argument applies.

Finally, we have:

{clm}

A deep proof system is needed to prove the formulas
R_{n}
.

Proof 5.7
.

This is proved by the same argument as in
[
Tiu06
]
. In order to
prove
R_{n}
, a
\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}
at depth
2n
has to be accessed first, in
order to remove the variable
y_{i}
(or
z_{i}
) with an atomic
interaction.

This strengthens Tiu’s argument by using formulas involving also
a
\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}
, showing that rules like entropy are not enough to obtain
a cut-free sequent style calculus for
{\mathsf{BV}}
.

Remark 67
.

However, Acclavio
[
Acc23
]
proposes a sequent system for a graphical logic (see also Section
6.1.5
) that includes a
“deep”
tensor rule, that in our setting would have the following shape:

{[\Gamma,A]}\qquad{[\Delta\{B\}]}

\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.36165pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}\text{-intro'}\;

{[\Gamma,\Delta\{A\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}B\}]}

With this rule,
[
Acc23
]
shows cut elimination in the sequent calculus for a logic that before had only a deep inference proof system. Naturally, we conjecture that something similar might be possible for
{\mathsf{BV}}
, but exploring this would have gone beyond the scope of this paper.

5.3.
A Complexity-Theoretic Obstacle to Sequent Calculi for Pomset Logic

Let us now turn to pomset logic. We propose here a similar (and somewhat
informal) complexity-theoretic explanation for why a cut-free sequent calculus
for pomset logic is difficult to find, similar to Remark
45
.

As described in
[
Ret21
, Section 5]
, the origins of pomset logic in
the coherence space semantics of linear logic suggest that
\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}
is meant to
be a
multiplicative
connective, just like the multiplicative conjunction

\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}
and the multiplicative disjunction
\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}
(which give their names to
the fragments
\mathsf{MLL}
and
\mathord{\mathsf{MLL\!+\!mix}}
of linear logic). Therefore, we would like to
leverage some proof-theoretic property related to multiplicative connectives.
Recall that the standard sequent calculus rules for
\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}
and for its

additive
counterpart
\&
correspond to two possible introduction rules
for the conjunction
\land
in classical logic, respectively:

\frac{\vdash\Gamma,A\quad\vdash\Delta,B}{\vdash\Gamma,\Delta,A\land B}\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\frac{\vdash\Gamma,A\quad\vdash\Gamma,B}{\vdash\Gamma,A\land B}

The difference is that the multiplicative rule
splits the context of the
conclusion into disjoint parts among the premises
while the additive rule
copies the same context in both premises. The same kind of management of the
context occurs in the rules for the so-called
“generalized
multiplicative connectives”
in the French-Italian linear logic
tradition
[
DR89
,
AM20
]
. This leads us to the following definition.

{defi}

A
multiplicative introduction rule
for an
n
-ary connective
\Phi
is
a rule whose instances obey the following property: there exist formulas

A_{1},\dots,A_{n}
and a multiset of formulas
M
such that

•

the multiset of formulas occurring in the premises --- i.e. the sum of the
multisets of formulas in each premise --- is equal to
24
24

24

We abusively
use the notation
\{\ldots\}
for a multiset rather than a set here.

\{A_{1},\dots,A_{n}\}+M
;

•

the multiset of formulas occurring in the conclusion equals

\Phi(A_{1},\dots,A_{n})+M
.

A
multiplicative structural rule
is one in which the premises (taken
together) and the conclusion have the same formulas, taking multiplicity into
account; this is an equality of multisets similar to the above.

A sequent calculus is
multiplicative
when all its rules are either an
axiom rule, a multiplicative introduction rule or a multiplicative structural
rule.

For this to make sense, there must be a way to associate to a sequent its
multiset of formulas. This works both for flat sequents — which are already
multisets — and for ordered sequents, and other kinds of generalized sequents
are conceivable. The standard
\mathord{\mathsf{MLL\!+\!mix}}
sequent calculus, as well as Retoré’s
calculus in the previous section and Slavnov’s calculus in the next one, are all
multiplicative.

The relevance of this notion to us is a consequence in the spirit of proof
complexity:

{clm}

In a proof in a multiplicative sequent calculus, the total number of
introduction rules is at most the total size of the formulas in the sequent
being proved.

In many cases, there will also be a bound on the structural rules in proofs. For
instance, the number of uses of multiplicative structural rules that require two
or more premises, such as the mix rule, is also linearly bounded by the size of the
conclusion formulas. Thus the following informal principle: a multiplicative
sequent calculus with
“reasonable”
structural rules admits proofs with a
polynomial number of inference rules.
25
25

25

This also applies to the
graphical proof system
of
[
AHS20
,
AHS22
]
that works on general graphs instead of cographs (which correspond to formulas, as discussed in Section
6.1.5
).

We also expect
the correctness of these proofs to hinge only on the local validity of their
inferences (unlike proof nets, whose global correctness criterion makes them
hard to check). According to a similar reasoning as Remark
45
,
then, if such a multiplicative calculus were to capture pomset logic, it would be impossible to
verify its inference rules in time polynomial in the size of the formulas,
unless
\mathbf{NP}=\mathbf{coNP}
. This is arguably a strong restriction on the design of
sequent calculi for pomset logic, such as the calculus in the next section.

5.4.
A Reconstruction of Slavnov’s Calculus

Let us now revisit the sound and complete proof system of
[
Sla19
]
for
pomset logic in light of our above remarks. It uses
decorated sequents
,
which are flat sequents (multisets of formulas) endowed with additional

“decorations”
(analogously to how Retoré’s sequents can be seen as flat
sequents decorated with series-parallel orders). The point is that in Slavnov’s
sequents, these decorations (defined in Definition
5.4
below)
take up most of the space; their size may be
exponentially bigger
than
the number of formulas in the sequent. This provides a good reason for inference
rule checking to be superpolynomial in the total size of formulas — this is
indeed necessary since this sequent calculus is, as we shall see, multiplicative
and
“reasonable”
in the above sense.

For the sake of completeness, we describe briefly here Slavnov’s system,
specialized to pomset logic. The paper
[
Sla19
]
also introduces and
deals with a related but different extension of
\mathsf{MLL}
called

“semicommutative
\mathsf{MLL}
”
, and it derives its sequent calculus for pomset
logic from the one for semicommutative
\mathsf{MLL}
. Our exposition avoids this
detour. We also aim at giving a high-level idea of what makes the system work.
We decompose our presentation of the system into three
“levels”

(inspired by the two-level treatment of
[
Sla19
]
).

First level: preproofs on flat sequents

First, consider the
“old-fashioned”
calculus on flat sequents whose
rules are as follows:

{\vbox{\hbox{\kern 23.78476pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\kern 13.66089pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{$$}}}\kern 13.66089pt}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\text{axiom}\;$}}$}}\vbox{\vbox to0.4pt{\vfill\hbox to27.32178pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt}\vfill}}\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{$$}}$}\hss}}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{$\kern 0.0pt\hbox{${[a,a^{\bot}]}$}\kern 0.0pt$}}}}}\kern 0.0pt}}}\qquad{\vbox{\hbox{\kern 15.8125pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{$\Gamma\quad\Delta$}}}}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\text{mix}\;$}}$}}\vbox{\vbox to0.4pt{\vfill\hbox to24.58339pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt}\vfill}}\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{$$}}$}\hss}\kern 0.0pt}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\kern 0.00002pt\hbox{\hbox{$\kern 0.0pt\hbox{${[\Gamma,\Delta]}$}\kern 0.0pt$}}\kern 0.00002pt}}}\kern 0.0pt}}}\qquad{\vbox{\hbox{\kern 42.52425pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\kern 8.33328pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{${[\Gamma,A]}\quad{[\Delta,B]}$}}}\kern 8.33328pt}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.36165pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}\text{-intro}\;$}}$}}\vbox{\vbox to0.4pt{\vfill\hbox to76.83676pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt}\vfill}}\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{$$}}$}\hss}}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{$\kern 0.0pt\hbox{${[\Gamma,\Delta,A\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}B]}$}\kern 0.0pt$}}}}}\kern 0.0pt}}}\qquad{\vbox{\hbox{\kern 30.64581pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\kern 3.88885pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{${[\Gamma,A,B]}$}}}\kern 3.88885pt}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\odot\text{-intro}\;$}}$}}\vbox{\vbox to0.4pt{\vfill\hbox to44.05896pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt}\vfill}}\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{$$}}$}\hss}}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{$\kern 0.0pt\hbox{${[\Gamma,A\odot B]}$}\kern 0.0pt$}}}}}\kern 0.0pt}}}\ \text{for}\ \odot\in\{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}},\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}\}

In other words, this system extends the usual cut-free sequent calculus for

\mathord{\mathsf{MLL\!+\!mix}}
with an introduction rule for
\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}
which is exactly the same as for

\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}
. Let us call
Slavnov pre-proofs
the derivation trees using these
rules. Obviously, some formulas that are not valid in pomset logic, such as

(a\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}b)\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}(a^{\bot}\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}b^{\bot})
, may admit Slavnov pre-proofs.

An important observation is that
there is a canonical map from Slavnov
pre-proofs to tree-like RB-prenets
that preserves the conclusion sequent. It
can be defined inductively by interpreting each of the inference rules as an
operation on proof nets in the obvious way; for instance the
\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}
-intro rule
corresponds to taking the union of two RB-prenets and adding a gadget for the
newly added
\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}
connective (cf. Figure
2
).

The goal of the
“second level”
of the proof system will then be to
filter out Slavnov pre-proofs that translate into correct tree-like RB-nets.

(For
\mathsf{MLL}
(resp.
\mathord{\mathsf{MLL\!+\!mix}}
) sequent calculus proofs, i.e. Slavnov pre-proofs
without the
\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}
-intro and mix rules (resp. the
\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}
-intro rule), it is
well-known that the result of the translation is a correct
\mathsf{MLL}
(resp.
\mathord{\mathsf{MLL\!+\!mix}}
) proof net: in those cases, it corresponds to a

“desequentialization”
operation whose idea goes back to Girard’s
original paper
[
Gir87
]
.)

Second level: decorations with
“multi-reachability”
information

At this stage, it is natural to add data that keeps track of paths in
RB-prenets, in order to reflect the correctness criterion.

{defi}

A
decoration
on a flat sequent
\Gamma
is a set
\mathfrak{d}
of sets of ordered
pairs of formula occurrences. The pair
(\Gamma,\mathfrak{d})
is then called a

decorated sequent
.

To each Slavnov pre-proof
\pi
of a flat sequent
\Gamma
, we associate a
decoration
\mathfrak{d}_{\pi}
— and thus the decorated sequent
\mathfrak{S}_{\pi}=(\Gamma,\mathfrak{d}_{\pi})
— as follows: first translate it into a tree-like
RB-prenet
\mathcal{G}
, and then let the set
\{(A_{1},B_{1}),\dots,(A_{n},B_{n})\}\in\mathfrak{d}_{\pi}

whenever there exists a family of
pairwise disjoint
æ-paths

(P_{i})_{i\in\{1,\dots,n\}}
such that for each
i
the path
P_{i}
goes from the conclusion vertex
of
\mathcal{G}
corresponding to
A_{i}
to the vertex corresponding to
B_{i}
.

The key property is now:

{clm}

The decorated sequent corresponding to a Slavnov-pre-proof is entirely
determined by the last rule and the decorated sequents corresponding to the
sub-pre-proofs of the premises.

For instance, there exists a function
\mathfrak{F}_{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.36165pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}}
such that given a proof

\pi
equal to

\pi_{1}

{[\Gamma,A]}

\pi_{2}

{[\Delta,B]}

\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.68887pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.68887pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.41331pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}
-intro

{[\Gamma,\Delta,A\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}B]}

we have
\mathfrak{S}_{\pi}=\mathfrak{F}_{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.36165pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}}(\mathfrak{S}_{\pi_{1}},\mathfrak{S}_{\pi_{2}},A,B)
.
26
26

26

Strictly speaking, the two last arguments point to formula occurrences in the
conclusions of
\pi_{1},\pi_{2}
.

To see why this claim holds, observe that
a family of disjoint æ-paths between conclusions in the tree-like RB-prenet
corresponding to
\pi
consists of the union of:

•

such a family in the prenet for
\pi_{1}
, not touching
A
;

•

such a family in the prenet for
\pi_{2}
, not touching
B
;

•

either the empty set, or a singleton containing one of the following:

–

an æ-path between (the vertex for) some formula occurrence in
\Gamma
and
some other in
\Delta
going through the RB-tree gadget for
A\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}B
;

–

an æ-path from some vertex from either
\Gamma
or
\Delta
to the
conclusion vertex for
A\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}B
;

–

the reverse of either of the above two possibilities.

An explicit expression for
\mathfrak{F}_{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.36165pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}}
can be obtained by reasoning along
these lines. Similar (simpler) analyses can be carried out for the other
connectives, and for the axiom and mix rules.

This allows us to lift each of the previous inference rules on flat sequents to
a rule on decorated sequents, for example the decorated version of

\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}
-intro would be

\frac{\quad\mathfrak{S}\qquad\qquad\qquad\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}\quad}{\mathfrak{F}_{\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.36165pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}}(\mathfrak{S},\mathfrak{S}^{\prime},A,B)}\qquad\text{where $A$ (resp.\ $B$) is a formula occurrence in $\mathfrak{S}$
(resp.\ $\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}$)}

These decorated inference rules can be read as a proof system, and we have:

{clm}

The derivation trees generated by those rules are exactly the ones that can be
obtained in the following way: start from a Slavnov pre-proof (with flat sequents) and for
each node, replace its value by
\mathfrak{S}_{\pi}
where
\pi
is the sub-pre-proof
rooted at that node.

Let us call these derivation trees
decorated pre-proofs
.

Third level: side condition using the decorations

At this point, we have obtained a new proof system that, in the end, proves the
same flat sequents as the former Slavnov pre-proofs. So it is still unsound with
respect to pomset logic. To remedy that, it remains to leverage the additional

“multi-reachability”
information provided by the decorations (in fact,
we use only reachability by a single æ-path between two formulas).

{clm}

A decorated pre-proof translates to a correct tree-like RB-net if and only if
we have
\{(B,A)\}\notin\mathfrak{d}
for every instance of a
\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}
-intro rule
in it of the form below:

\frac{({[\Gamma,A,B]},\mathfrak{d})}{({[\Gamma,A\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}B]},\mathfrak{d}^{\prime})}

Proof 5.8
.

In the inductive translation of Slavnov pre-proofs to prenets, if the last
rule is any other than
\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}
-intro, then the sub-pre-proofs for its premises
are all mapped to correct nets if and only if the translation of the whole
pre-proof is itself a correct net. In fact, this is precisely why the
desequentialization of
\mathord{\mathsf{MLL\!+\!mix}}
sequent proofs into proof nets is sound, a
well-known fact. However, for a
\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}
-intro rule (using the above
notations), the corresponding operation on tree-like RB-prenets may create new
æ-cycles. From the shape of the RB-tree gadget associated to
\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}
(cf. Figure
2
) one can see that such new cycles can only be
composed of the directed edge of this gadget from
A
to
B
plus an æ-path
from
B
to
A
in the prenet for the premise. The existence of the latter is
precisely equivalent to
\{(B,A)\}\in\mathfrak{d}
.

Note that the assumption in the above claim only consists in purely local

“side conditions”
on inference rules, hence:

{defi}

A
decorated proof
is a derivation tree in the system whose inference
rules are those of decorated pre-proofs except that the
\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}
-intro rule is
subject to a
side condition
: it can only be applied to

({[\Gamma,A,B]},\mathfrak{d})
(to introduce
A\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}B
) when

\{(B,A)\}\notin\mathfrak{d}
.

The
“if”
part of Claim
5.4
can then be
rephrased as the
soundness of the decorated proof system
for pomset logic.

Let us also sketch briefly a
completeness
argument. Given a correct
pomset logic proof net
\mathcal{G}
, first consider a copy
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
where every
\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}
has been
turned into
\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}
. This is still a correct net (replacing
\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}
by
\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}

removes an edge, so it can destroy æ-cycles, not create them), in fact
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
is
an
\mathord{\mathsf{MLL\!+\!mix}}
proof net. There exists an
\mathord{\mathsf{MLL\!+\!mix}}
sequent proof whose inductive
translation (desequentialization) is
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
— this is the

sequentialization theorem
(a result involving non-trivial combinatorics,
originating in
[
Gir87
]
; see
[
Ret03
,
Ngu20
]
for a discussion
of its
“equivalence”
with earlier results in mainstream graph theory).
Next, in this sequentialization, replace the relevant occurrences of
\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}
by

\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}
; we obtain a Slavnov pre-proof (since in this system the rule for

\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}
is the same as the
\mathord{\mathsf{MLL\!+\!mix}}
rule for
\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}
) which lifts uniquely to a
decorated pre-proof. Finally, one must check that the latter satisfies the side
conditions; this comes from the correctness of the pomset logic proof net
\mathcal{G}
that we
started with, plus the
“only if”
part of
Claim
5.4
.

6.
Conclusion

In the first paper of this series
[
Gug07
]
, Guglielmi announced the task of
the present one in the following way:

It is still open whether the logic in this paper, called
{\mathsf{BV}}
, is the same as
pomset logic. We conjecture that it is actually the same logic, but one
crucial step is still missing, at the time of this writing, in the equivalence
proof. This paper is the first in a planned series of three papers dedicated
to
{\mathsf{BV}}
. […] In the third part, some of my colleagues will hopefully show
the equivalence of
{\mathsf{BV}}
and pomset logic, this way explaining why it was
impossible to find a sequent system for pomset logic.

Surprisingly, the hoped-for equivalence turned out to be false; in

Section

3
, we exhibited an explicit formula provable in pomset logic,
but not in
{\mathsf{BV}}
. What first led us to seek such a counter-example was the
discovery of the complexity-theoretic hardness results of
Section

4
,
according to which the conjectured equivalence would have implied
\mathbf{NP}=\mathbf{coNP}
.
This, plus Slavnov’s recent system
[
Sla19
]
, put into question the
established narrative about the impossibility of sequent calculi for pomset
logic, so we revisited this topic in
Section

5
(and showed in passing
that an old sequent calculus with cuts was in fact equivalent to
{\mathsf{BV}}
).

6.1.
Related topics

As we hope that this paper may serve as a reference for readers who wish to get
acquainted with
{\mathsf{BV}}
and pomset logic (hence the lengthy
Section

2
), we
will broadly survey here some works that are connected to these two systems,
without limiting ourselves to provability or complexity-theoretic aspects.

6.1.1.
Applications and semantics of
{\mathsf{BV}}
and pomset logic

One of the first applications of self-dual non-commutativity was Reddy’s Linear
Logic Model of State
[
Red93
]
. This work, whose ultimate goal is to study
mutable state in programming languages, introduces
\mathsf{LLMS}
, which is an extension
of intuitionistic
27
27

27

In the context of linear logic,

“intuitionistic”
means that the sequents are two-sided with the right
side being limited to a single formula. The sequents in
[
Red93
]
are of
the form
\Gamma\vdash A
where
\Gamma
is what we call an ordered sequent.

linear logic with some connectives, one of which is
\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}
.
\mathsf{LLMS}

comes with a semantics in coherence spaces where the interpretation of
\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}

coincides with that for pomset logic. The proof system for
\mathsf{LLMS}
is a
sequent calculus similar to Retoré’s one (§
5.1
). It also
admits a semantics in Dialectica categories
[
dP14
, §4]
.

As for the proof nets of pomset logic, they have no known notion of categorical
semantics; in fact, in light of the connections between categorical logic and
deep inference
[
Hug04
]
, it might be argued that any presentation of
pomset logic as an
“initial something-category”
would amount to giving a
deductive proof system for it. However the same connections make modeling
{\mathsf{BV}}

categorically a straightforward matter, as has been done in
[
BPS12
]
where
a new concrete semantics (probabilistic coherence spaces) is also given as an
example of
{\mathsf{BV}}
-category. There have been recent works relating
{\mathsf{BV}}
and

{\mathsf{BV}}
-categories to quantum causality
[
BGI+14
,
SK22
]
.

Finally, let us mention that Retoré and his collaborators have applied pomset
logic to mathematical linguistics (see
[
Ret21
, Section 7]
). This
provides an alternative to the usual approach in
categorial
grammars

[
MR12
]
which relies on the another kind of
non-commutative logic that we shall cover next.

6.1.2.
Other non-commutative variants of linear logic

Linearity and non-commutativity first appeared in the study of typed

\lambda
-calculi in the Lambek calculus
[
Lam58
]
, whose introduction
was motivated by the aforementioned linguistic applications. We might thus
consider it retrospectively as the first non-commutative logic, even though the

formulas-as-types correspondence
28
28

28

Also known as

“proofs-as-programs”
or
“Curry–Howard”
correspondence.

between typed
\lambda
-calculi and constructive logics was not known at the
time. In the Lambek calculus, the order of arguments of a function matters: thus

A\multimap B\multimap C\not\equiv B\multimap A\multimap C
where

\multimap
is the linear implication connective.

In a classical linear logic framework, where
A\multimap B
may be defined as

A^{\bot}\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}B
, this translates into
A\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}B\not\equiv B\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}A
– a
non-commutativity in the literal sense. This entails the non-commutativity of
its dual connective
\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}
and we have
(A\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}B)^{\bot}=B^{\bot}\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}A^{\bot}
. (In contrast, pomset logic keeps
\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}},\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}
commutative while
adding the new connective
\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}
, and the self-duality of
\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}
does

not
permute its arguments.) The standard system with those properties is

cyclic
29
29

29

This is entirely unrelated to the so-called
“cyclic
proofs”
(also known as
“circular proofs”
) for logics with fixed
points or (co)inductive definitions, a trendy research topic at the time of
writing.
linear logic
; see
[
Yet90
]
for its
sequent calculus and proof nets,
[
DG04
]
for a deep inference system
and
[
AM19
]
for pointers to more recent work on cyclic
\mathsf{MLL}
.

On
\lambda
-terms or proof nets, non-commutativity corresponds to a

planarity
condition; to our knowledge, this was first remarked by Girard
in
[
Gir89
, Section II.9]
just after his discovery of linear
logic
[
Gir87
]
. For more recent works pursuing such topological ideas,
see e.g.
[
APR05
,
Abr07
,
Mel18
]
. In particular, renewed
interest in the non-commutative linear
\lambda
-calculus has come from the
discovery of a deep connection with the combinatorics of planar
maps
[
ZG15
]
, including bijective and enumerative aspects.

The aforementioned works consider proofs or
\lambda
-terms as static
combinatorial objects, but they can also be seen as programs. In this
perspective, unexpected computational consequences of non-commutativity in the

\lambda
-calculus have recently been uncovered in an automata-theoretic
setting
[
NP20
]
.

Finally, let us mention Abrusci and Ruet’s logic
[
AR99
,
Rue00
]

where commutative and non-commutative versions of the connectives
\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}
and
\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}

coexist.

6.1.3.
Proof nets vs denotational semantics

Pomset logic comes from trying to extract a syntactic correctness criterion from
the coherence space semantics: the interpretation of a pre-proof net in
coherence spaces can be defined by means of so-called experiments, and we want
the result of the experiments to be a valid member of the semantics. (To be
precise, we want the set of points obtained by experiments to form a clique.)
For
\mathord{\mathsf{MLL\!+\!mix}}
proof nets, Retoré showed that this condition is equivalent to
correctness
[
Ret97b
]
, and the correctness criterion for pomset proof nets
was designed to extend this correspondence (this is discussed
in
[
Ret97a
]
).

Pagani has applied a similar methodology to
\mathsf{MELL}

(Multiplicative-Exponential Linear Logic) pre-proof nets: he shows
in
[
Pag06
]
that the validity of coherence space experiments — using
a certain
“non-uniform”
interpretation of the exponentials — is
equivalent to a certain graph-theoretical condition,
visible acyclicity
,
which is weaker than the usual correctness criterion for

\mathord{\mathsf{MELL\!+\!mix}}
. This is later extended to differential
interaction nets in
[
Pag12
]
; since coherence spaces are not a
semantics of differential linear logic, the result of
[
Pag12
]
is
formulated with respect to Ehrhard’s finiteness spaces instead.

A similarity between correctness for pomset proof nets and visible acyclicity is
that both involve
directed
edges and cycles. Thanks to this, it is
straightforward to show that visible acyclicity is
\mathbf{coNP}
-hard, by adapting
the proof for pomset logic; however we do not know whether, conversely, it is in

\mathbf{coNP}
.

Let us also mention Tranquili’s
hypercorrectness
criterion for
\mathsf{MALL}

(Multiplicative-Additive) pre-proof nets, coming from their semantics in
hypercoherences
[
Tra08
]
. Here again the condition
obtained is weaker than the usual correctness criterion — so there are
hypercorrect
\mathsf{MALL}
pre-proof nets that are not sequentializable. (Coherence
spaces would allow even more non-sequentializable pre-proof nets, for instance
a version of Berry’s famous
“Gustave function”
.)

6.1.4.
Extensions of
{\mathsf{BV}}

Given that
{\mathsf{BV}}
and pomset logic are
“multiplicative”
logics, it is
natural to make extensions with other primitives, like the additives
and exponentials of linear logic, or other modalities or quantifiers.

This has indeed been done, but so far only for
{\mathsf{BV}}
. The first such extension was adding the exponential of linear logic to
{\mathsf{BV}}
, leading to the logic
\mathsf{NEL}
, which is studied in the fourth and fifth paper of this series
[
SG11
,
GS11
]
.

In
[
Rov16
]
, Roversi adds a self-dual binder to
{\mathsf{BV}}
, in order to establish a correspondence to the linear
\lambda
-calculus, in the spirit of the
formulas-as-types
paradigm.

The next natural extension was adding the additives, leading to the logic
\mathsf{MAV}

[
Hor15
]
, which has then been extended by nominal quantifiers (and standard first-order quantifiers)
[
HTAC19
,
HT19
]
in order to simulate private names in process algebras, as for example the
\pi
-calculus. This is in the line of research by Bruscoli
[
Bru02
]
who used
{\mathsf{BV}}
to simulate reductions in
\mathsf{CCS}
, following the
formulas-as-processes
paradigm.

6.1.5.
Beyond formulas

The formulas-as-processes paradigm has recently motivated another line of research. The restrictions on digraphs, that define dicographs which correspond to formulas (see Definitions
2.2
,
2.2
and Theorem
8
) and that therefore make proof theory possible in the first place, are also an obstacle to the formulas-as-processes paradigm because there are processes that do contain the forbidden configurations in (
2
) in Definition
2.2
, and do therefore not correspond to formulas.
This suggests to define a proof system directly on the graphs instead of the formulas, and use the modular decomposition tree instead of the formula tree. This idea (first briefly mentioned in
[
NS18
, Section 5]
) has been explored in
[
AHS20
,
AHS22
]
and
[
CDW20
]
for undirected graphs and in
[
AHMS22
]
for digraphs. It turns out that if we drop the cograph/dicograph condition, there is a much larger space of possible proof systems, that still waits to be explored.

6.2.
Open problems

For a long time, it was believed that there was a canonical extension of
\mathord{\mathsf{MLL\!+\!mix}}

with the connective
\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}
that had both a deductive proof system (
{\mathsf{BV}}
) and
proof nets with a simple correctness criterion (pomset logic). Now that we have
refuted this, several questions arise:

•

We might want to design truly well-behaved deductive proofs for pomset
logic — given the obstructions that we have seen in this paper, this looks
challenging. Slavnov’s sequent calculus is a start, but it is not clear for us
whether a cut-elimination procedure can be defined directly on it without
going through a translation into proof nets. And even without insisting on the
proof system being deductive, the requirement of tractable proof checking
rules out proof nets by themselves (Remark
47
).

•

Our results might also be interpreted as suggesting that of the two
logics,
{\mathsf{BV}}
was the
“right”
one all along. Then it would be
desirable to have a system of proof nets for
{\mathsf{BV}}
. Perhaps it suffices to
extend the correctness criterion of pomset logic so that it excludes more
pre-proofs. If that were the case, then the problem of

“
{\mathsf{BV}}
-correctness”
of pre-proof nets would be equivalent to the

{\mathsf{BV}}
-provability problem for balanced formulas. This also raises the question
of the complexity of the latter:
\mathbf{NP}
-completeness would rule out
\mathbf{coNP}

critera of the sort
“there does not exist some bad structure (e.g. some kind of cycle) in the pre-proof net”
. Alternatively, the right notion
of proof net could involve not just the formula tree and axiom linking, but
some extra structure too (maybe an order on the axiom linking?); there are
some precedents for this in the theory of
\mathsf{MLL}
proof nets with units (with
many variants, recapitulated in
[
Hug12
, Table 1]
).

•

More generally, now that we have two logics that (i) are built
from the connectives
\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\bindnasrepma$}}},\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\triangleleft$}}},\mathchoice{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.86108pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\raise 0.51663pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}{\mathbin{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\varotimes$}}}
, (ii) are conservative over

\mathord{\mathsf{MLL\!+\!mix}}
, and (iii) admit cut elimination, the question arises whether
these are the only two or whether there is a hierarchy of such logics
with increasing proof complexity.

During the research for this paper, another interesting question arose.
We conjecture the following generalization of the
construction of the formula in
Section

3
: given a balanced tautology
of classical logic, one can always
“make the axiom links directed”
in
some way (cf. Remark
37
) to get a provable formula in pomset
logic.
